A common thread runs through Bologna's entire scholarly output: the historiographical vindication of the art of southern Italy, traditionally neglected by criticism centered on Florence and Rome. His study of the ceiling of the Sala Magna at the Steri in Palermo (The Ceiling of the Sala Magna at the Steri in Palermo and Sicilian Feudal Culture in the Late Middle Ages, 1975, now in its fourth edition) is a paradigmatic example of this approach: Bologna decodes the complex figurative program of the 14th-century ceiling as a mirror of Sicilian feudal culture at the moment of its decline, interweaving art history, political history, and the history of mentalities with a methodology that was pioneering at the time. Similarly, his studies on Francesco Solimena (1958), on Roviale Spagnolo and 16th-Century Neapolitan Painting (1959), and on Gaspare Traversi in the European Enlightenment (1980) demonstrate a constant focus on modern Neapolitan art, in the conviction that it deserved the same critical standing reserved for the northern schools.

Bologna was also a theorist of historiographical method, author of some of the most stimulating contributions on the problem of art history as a discipline. In the volume From the Minor Arts to Industrial Design: History of an Ideology (1972), he investigated the genesis of the concept of "decorative arts" and its ideological function within the capitalist system, anticipating issues that would become central to the international debate of the 1980s. With Methods of Studying Italian Art and the Methodological Problem Today (1979, for Einaudi's Storia dell'arte) and La coscienza storica dell'arte d'Italia (1982), he took a clear stand against what he called Warburgian-derived iconological "mentalism"—the, in his view, casual use of iconology as an interpretive key independent of the material history of the works. He edited the ambitious UTET series Storia dell'arte in Italia (eight volumes, 1980–1988), which remains a standard reference work in Italian university libraries.

In the 1990s, Bologna explored the theme of naturalism in painting with the volume L'incredulità del Caravaggio e l'esperienza delle "cose naturali" (1992), in which he interpreted Caravaggio's realism not as an individual aesthetic choice but as a response to a collective cultural need of post-Tridentine Catholicism, rooted in the tangible concreteness of Franciscan preaching and in the Lombard tradition of observing nature. He also curated the exhibition Battistello Caracciolo and Early Naturalism in Naples (1991), a further contribution to the appreciation of the Neapolitan Caravaggio period. Ferdinando Bologna passed away on April 3, 2019 in Ocre, Abruzzo, at the age of ninety-four, leaving behind a scientific library of extraordinary scope and a school of students who have continued his southern-focused approach to the history of Italian art.

Boskovits, Miklós

Budapest, 1935 – 2011

Hungarian-born Italian art historian renowned for his rigorous scholarship on Tuscan painting from the Duecento through the early Trecento, establishing himself as a pivotal figure in the attribution of anonymous masters and the refinement of chronologies of Sienese and Florentine primitives. Born in Budapest on October 11, 1935, Boskovits fled Hungary amid the 1956 revolution and settled in Italy, where he pursued classical studies and art history at the Università Cattolica del Sacro Cuore in Milan. Working within the intellectual legacy of Pietro Toesca and under the influence of Cesare Brandi, he developed a method that blended connoisseurship, archival rigour, and close stylistic analysis. He later held a professorship at the same university, where he taught courses on Giotto and the Florentine primitives.

Boskovits revolutionised attributions in medieval Tuscan painting and worked in close collaboration with Luciano Bellosi as one of the foremost experts on the period. Their analyses underpinned the reattribution of Cimabue's Castelfiorentino Madonna (c. 1283–1284), distinguishing it from Duccio's Crevola Madonna through nuanced comparisons of drapery, halo treatment, and iconographic detail. His seminal article Su Niccolò di Buonaccorso, Benedetto di Bindo and Sienese Painting in the Early Fifteenth Century (Paragone, 1980) dissected Trecento Sienese developments, while broader studies on Bartolomeo Bulgarini and Niccolò di Buonaccorso clarified workshop practices and stylistic evolutions in the aftermath of the Black Death. His oeuvre emphasised the interplay of Florentine innovation — Giotto's naturalism — with Sienese Gothic elegance, reshaping understandings of figures such as the Master of the Bambino Vispo.

As professor emeritus at the Università Cattolica, Boskovits contributed to catalogues for major exhibitions and institutions, influencing the restoration and museum display of Duecento–Trecento panels. His methodology bridged Brandi's restoration theory with the iconographic precision associated with Carlo Bertelli, as seen in collaborative contexts on Lombard and Sienese heritage. His multi-volume Italian Paintings before 1400 for the National Gallery of Art in Washington remains a fundamental reference tool. He died in 2011, leaving a corpus that continues to guide scholars navigating the anonymous masters of medieval Tuscany.

Bourke, Cormac

Dublin, active late 20th-21st century

Cormac Bourke is an Irish archaeologist born in Dublin, specialising in medieval studies, early church history, and insular Christianity. His career has been defined by an unusually sharp methodological focus: rather than broad historical surveys, he works at the precise intersection of material culture and religious practice, reading saints' relics, liturgical objects, and metalwork as primary historical documents. He is currently based in Belfast and is recognised as one of the foremost authorities on early medieval Irish sacred objects.

Bourke served for twenty-six years as Curator of Medieval Antiquities at the Ulster Museum, Belfast, a tenure that gave him unparalleled hands-on access to the collections of early insular metalwork and relics held in Northern Ireland. Following his retirement from the Ulster Museum, he moved to the antiquities department of the National Museum of Ireland, where he has continued his active research and curatorial work. In both institutions he has combined object-based scholarship with public engagement, participating in lectures and interpretive projects that bring early medieval material culture to wider audiences.

The intellectual core of Bourke's work is the archaeology of saints' relics and the devotional objects associated with them. His publications range across early Irish hand-bells, insular croziers, Celtic brooches, crucifixion plaques, and cumdachs (elaborate book-shrines). His magnum opus in this field is The Early Medieval Hand-bells of Ireland and Britain (Wordwell Books, 2020; revised 2022), a 708-page study that the National Museum of Ireland described as "the most authoritative study ever undertaken on medieval hand-bells" and "an immense achievement both nationally and internationally". Hand-bells were emblematic objects of early Irish monastic life, and Bourke's exhaustive catalogue and analysis of surviving examples transformed a relatively under-studied category of object into a richly documented chapter in the history of insular Christianity.

In the field of Columban scholarship specifically, Bourke edited the landmark volume Studies in the Cult of Saint Columba (Four Courts Press, 1997), which brought together twelve original essays by leading specialists covering the saint's life, the history of Iona, and the wider Columban monastic federation. He has also published directly on Adomnan's Vita Columbae, including the article "A play on a letter in Adomnan's Life of Saint Columba" (Innes Review, 2019), which examines a subtle textual detail within the primary hagiographic source. His essay "Peregrinatio Columbae" (2004) explored the theology and practice of holy pilgrimage in the Columban tradition, contributing to the broader debate about Irish peregrinatio pro Christo as a form of spiritual exile.

One of the more distinctive threads in Bourke's scholarship is his attention to the transmission of Irish sacred objects beyond Ireland and Britain. His article "The early Irish reliquary in Bobbio" (Archivum Bobiense, 1994-95) examined an Irish relic container preserved at the monastery of Bobbio in northern Italy, founded by St. Columbanus - a find of direct relevance to the study of Irish monastic influence on the Continent. His work on "Clonmore and Bobbio: two seventh-century shrines" (2001-02) extended this line of inquiry, comparing Irish shrine-making traditions with surviving Lombard-era parallels. More recently, in 2025, he published a major article in the Proceedings of the Royal Irish Academy reinterpreting the Shrine of St. Brigid's Shoe, a metalwork object from County Galway that had been misunderstood since the eighteenth century, providing its first complete description and historical interpretation.

Though Bourke's primary expertise is material rather than textual, his scholarship intersects meaningfully with the world of illuminated manuscripts. His 2024 article "Stolen by night in 1007: a crux in the history of the Book of Kells" (Riocht na Midhe, 2024) addressed a key episode in the transmission history of the Columban codex, re-examining the documentary evidence for the famous theft of the manuscript. This work situates him as a valuable voice in the broader scholarly debate about the Book of Kells' origins and custody within the Columban monastic network.

His article output has appeared consistently in the Ulster Journal of Archaeology, the Proceedings of the Royal Irish Academy, the Journal of the Royal Society of Antiquaries of Ireland, and in major edited volumes published by Brill, Four Courts Press, and Yale University Press. He contributed the entry on St. Patrick to the Dictionary of Irish Biography (Royal Irish Academy / Cambridge University Press, 2009), and the entry on liturgical staves to The Eerdmans Encyclopedia of Early Christian Art and Archaeology (2017). His Academia.edu profile lists over seventy-seven research papers and has accumulated more than 15,000 public views, placing him in the top 3% of researchers on the platform.

Brandi, Cesare

Siena, 1906 – Vignano, 1988

Born on April 8, 1906, in Siena, Brandi graduated in literature from the University of Florence in 1928, following studies that also included law at the University of Siena. He began his career in the Administration of Antiquities and Fine Arts, starting in Siena in 1930 where he catalogued paintings for the Academy of Fine Arts, and later moving to Bologna in 1933 as an inspector, during which he organised early restoration workshops and exhibitions such as the Exhibition of Riminese Painting of the Fourteenth Century in 1935. These formative roles immersed him in practical conservation challenges, shaping his later theoretical innovations amid Italy's rich medieval and Renaissance heritage.

In 1939, Brandi became the inaugural director of the Istituto Centrale per il Restauro (Central Institute for Restoration) in Rome — a position he held until 1959 — transforming it into a global benchmark for conservation science. Proposed for the role by fellow critic Giulio Carlo Argan, he designed the institute to integrate phenomenological philosophy with hands-on techniques, addressing postwar damages to frescoes in places like Mantua and Viterbo. His tenure extended to UNESCO collaborations from 1948, international consultancies, and the role of Superintendent of Antiquities and Fine Arts from 1953, earning honours such as the Feltrinelli Prize for art criticism in 1958, shared with Argan.

Brandi's enduring legacy stems from his theory of restoration, first outlined in the 1963 book Teoria del Restauro, which posits that restoration must respect an artwork's historical authenticity, aesthetic unity, and material integrity — often summarised as the "duality of matter and form." Drawing from philosophers including Heidegger, Husserl, Hegel, and Croce, he argued for interventions that reveal rather than replace the object's temporal patina, influencing Italy's 1972 restoration charter and worldwide conservation practice. Major publications such as Le due vie (1966) and Teoria generale della critica (1974) further explored aesthetics and criticism, sparking debates with thinkers including Roland Barthes.

As a professor of medieval and modern art history — first as a free lecturer at the University of Rome from 1934, then holding chairs at Palermo (1961) and Rome's La Sapienza — Brandi mentored generations of scholars while producing essays on artists such as Filippo de Pisis and catalogues of Tuscan collections. He founded journals including L'Immagine (1947–1950) and the institute's Bollettino, blending criticism with travelogues and poetry that reflected his phenomenological approach to art's encounter with time. He died on January 19, 1988, in Vignano, leaving an adopted son, Vittorio Brandi Rubiu, who also pursued art history.

Brown, Michelle P.

b. 1959

Distinguished British scholar specialising in medieval manuscripts, Professor Emerita of Medieval Manuscript Studies at the School of Advanced Study, University of London, and formerly Curator of Illuminated Manuscripts at the British Library (1986–2004), where she managed, preserved, and promoted access to the Library's holdings of illuminated manuscripts, including contributions to digitisation projects. At the University of London she taught on the History of the Book MA programme and supervised doctoral research on palaeography, book history, and medieval art; she has also held visiting professorships at University College London and Durham University, and was awarded the Sandars Readership in Bibliography at Cambridge (2008–2009). Additional roles include Lay Canon at St Paul's Cathedral and director of the Hereford Mappa Mundi Trust; she remains active as an independent scholar.

Her research spans the transition from Late Antiquity to the Early Middle Ages — the Conversion period, biblical and liturgical manuscripts, Anglo-Saxon and Celtic cultures, and early East–West relations, including ongoing work on manuscripts at St Catherine's Monastery on Sinai. Her principal publications include Understanding Illuminated Manuscripts (1994), The Lindisfarne Gospels: Society, Spirituality and the Scribe (2003) — the standard monographic treatment of Eadfrith's manuscript, which established the eremitic devotional interpretation of its creation — The Luttrell Psalter facsimile (2006), Manuscripts from the Anglo-Saxon Age (2008), and Bede and the Theory of Everything (2021). A Fellow of the Society of Antiquaries (FSA), she also works as a historical consultant and on-screen expert for radio and television, making manuscript studies accessible to broader audiences.

Burresi, Mariagiulia

active late 20th-21st century

Mariagiulia Burresi is one of the leading historians of medieval art in central Italy, recognized nationally for her studies on painting, sculpture, and the artistic heritage of Pisa between the twelfth and fifteenth centuries.

Burresi earned a degree in Humanities with a specialization in Art History from the University of Pisa in 1970, graduating with highest honors. She furthered her academic training with a postgraduate course in Art History at the University of Florence between 1974 and 1975. Between 1968 and 1976, she taught at the Ministry of Education.

In 1977, she began her long career as Coordinating Director of Art History at the Ministry of Cultural Heritage and Activities, a role she held for over thirty years, until 2010. In this capacity, she served as an official at the Superintendence for Artistic and Historical Heritage of Pisa and Livorno and held the position of Director of the National Museum of San Matteo in Pisa, one of the most important museums for medieval Tuscan painting. In 2011-2012, she was again called upon as an expert consultant by the same Superintendence.

Her scholarly work has focused primarily on medieval Pisan art, with particular attention to mural painting, Gothic sculpture, religious architecture, and art collections. One of her most celebrated contributions is the monograph Santa Maria della Spina in Pisa (Silvana Editoriale, Milan, 1990), in which she addressed, with philological rigor, the issues related to the construction phases of this extraordinary Gothic chapel, combining historical-critical analysis with previously unpublished photographic documentation.

In collaboration with Antonino Caleca, an internationally renowned scholar, Burresi edited the volume Medieval Frescoes in Pisa (2003), a fundamental resource on the city's mural painting featuring the publication of numerous previously unpublished works. Together with Caleca, she also edited the catalog for the exhibition Cimabue in Pisa: 13th-Century Pisan Painting from Giunta to Giotto (2005), which brought together over a hundred works of painting, sculpture, and goldsmithing from thirteenth-century Pisa at the Museum of San Matteo.

Burresi served as editor of numerous exhibition catalogs of high scholarly merit. In 1983, she edited Andrea, Nino, and Tommaso: Pisan Sculptors (Pontedera), dedicated to the great masters of Pisan Gothic sculpture. In 1992, she edited Nel secolo di Lorenzo: restauri di opere d'arte del Quattrocento. She then worked on Il modello e la copia: dai quadroni del Duomo di Pisa tra Settecento e Ottocento (1993-1994, Museo dell'Opera del Duomo) and the essay Alla ricerca di un'identità: le pubbliche collezioni d'arte a Pisa fra Settecento e Novecento (1999), a contribution of great historiographical interest on the reception of the Pisan "primitives" in public collections.

Also noteworthy is her editing of the Inventario dei beni artistici nel Compartimento di Pisa (1860-1863) by the painter Marianini, published in 2007, a nineteenth-century inventory of extraordinary documentary value for reconstructing the original locations of many works that were later dispersed. In 2017, she edited the volume Di antichissima fondazione: il Palazzo del Governo di Pisa (Pacini Editore), confirming her commitment to promoting the city's architectural heritage.

Her contribution to the historiography of medieval Pisa is recognized as indispensable by the academic community. Laura Fenelli, in the critical review Fifteen Years of Art-Historical Studies on the Medieval Period in Pisa published in Predella, ranks Burresi among the leading figures in research on medieval art in Pisa, emphasizing how her works have "for decades shed light on key moments in the history of medieval art in the city." The CNR journal RiMe cites her in connection with studies on the Sacre Passioni and the Deposition of Christ in Pisa Cathedral, attesting to the iconographic breadth of her research.

Burresi thus embodied with rare consistency the dual vocation of the art historian in the service of both scientific research and the public preservation of heritage, leaving a lasting mark on the knowledge and safeguarding of medieval art in Pisa.

C

Caleca, Antonino

Italian scholar of art history and visual culture, particularly active in the study of medieval and Renaissance Tuscan art, image theory, and the history of Pisa. Describing himself as a researcher in art history and in the philosophy of visual language, he combines close empirical work on monuments, paintings, and objects with sustained reflection on how images shape perception and meaning.

Caleca began his professional life in heritage and museum practice, serving as conservator of artistic heritage in western Tuscany, especially in the province of Pisa. This institutional experience gave him direct and prolonged contact with collections, archives, and local cultural bodies, and it informed the curatorial and archival sensitivity visible throughout his later publications. In the early 2000s he also taught in the Department of Communication Science at the University of Pisa and later at the University of Siena, where he offered courses in art history and urban history that linked visual form to broader social and political processes.

His scholarship spans several connected fields: Pisan Romanesque and Gothic architecture; civic and religious monuments as instruments of power, identity, and urban memory; and the visual construction of the city through maps, views, and narrative imagery from the Middle Ages to the early modern period. He has also studied objects and ornament, and has addressed the philosophical and hermeneutic implications of visual representation, particularly in relation to memory, mediation, and the image of the city. In the 1990s he served as co-director of Critica d'Arte and later its new series, collaborating with Giuseppe Cuscito and Giovanni C. F. Villa; as co-director of the Storia dell'arte series (Viella, Rome), he helped shape volumes that connect Italian and international scholarship on the long duration of image-making in Italy and the Mediterranean. Alongside journal essays and conference papers, he has contributed to exhibition catalogues and collective volumes on Tuscan art, establishing a profile that moves fluently between museum practice, urban visual history, and theory of the image.

Cannon, Joanna

active late 20th-21st century

Joanna Cannon is a British art historian and professor whose work has profoundly shaped the study of late medieval Italian art, especially the painting and architecture of thirteenth- and fourteenth-century Italy. She has been a central figure at the Courtauld Institute of Art in London for decades, influencing several generations of scholars working on communal Italy, mendicant orders, and the intersection of art, spirituality and technical analysis.

Joanna Cannon studied both as an undergraduate and a postgraduate at the Courtauld Institute of Art, completing her PhD there in 1980. She began teaching at the Courtauld in 1977, an unusually early appointment that underlines how quickly she established herself as a specialist, and she has remained there throughout her career. In 2014 she was appointed Professor in the History of Art, consolidating her long-standing role in the institution's teaching and research culture. Within the Courtauld she served as Head of the Classical, Byzantine and Medieval Section from 2004 to 2008 and later as Head of Examinations between 2012 and 2014, roles that reflect her leadership in curriculum and institutional governance.

Her influence extends beyond formal titles. She has been a convenor of the seminar group "Giotto's Circle," an important forum for medieval art historians, and has been heavily involved in organizing research events, including acting as local organizer of the annual "ICMA at The Courtauld" lecture and co-organizing workshops such as "Art, Architecture and the Indulgence." A festschrift, "Late Medieval Italian Art and Its Contexts: Essays in Honour of Professor Joanna Cannon," attests to the depth of her impact on her students and colleagues, many of whom contributed essays reflecting the breadth of topics she has helped open up in early Italian art history.

Cannon's research focuses on the art and architecture of Italy in the thirteenth and fourteenth centuries, with a strong emphasis on the ways visual evidence can be used as a historical source. She is particularly interested in how historical circumstances - political, social, religious - shape the formal development of art, and conversely how artistic forms can illuminate civic religion and lay spirituality in the Italy of the communes. Another hallmark of her work is the integration of technical art history into interpretive narratives: she has been concerned with how detailed knowledge of materials, techniques and physical condition can inform questions of dating, authorship and function.

This methodological orientation has led her to collaborate closely with conservators and scientists. Together with Aviva Burnstock and Caroline Campbell she directed an Andrew W. Mellon Foundation-funded project to create a research website on the Master of the Fogg Pieta/Maestro di Figline, focusing on technical aspects of works attributed to this problematic figure across international collections. She is also a trustee of the Caroline Villers Research Fellowship, which specifically promotes research in technical art history, reinforcing her commitment to bridging connoisseurship, historical inquiry and scientific investigation.

Among Cannon's most important publications is the monograph Religious Poverty, Visual Riches, published in 2013, which examines how richly endowed visual programmes coexisted with and articulated ideals of religious poverty in late medieval Italy. This work has been described as seminal in showing how images and architectural settings mediated civic religion and religious experience, especially among the laity, emphasizing the role of visual culture in communal devotion. Her overall output spans more than fifty publications over roughly six decades, consistently returning to the intersection of art and spirituality.

More recently, she has been closely associated with the research and exhibition project Siena: The Rise of Painting, 1300-1350, undertaken with Caroline Campbell and Stephan Wolohojian for the National Gallery, London. This project, culminating in a 2024-2025 catalog and exhibition, explores a crucial turning point in Italian art through masterpieces by Duccio, Simone Martini, the Lorenzetti brothers and their contemporaries, situating Sienese painting within broader artistic and devotional contexts. Reviews highlight its "important new scholarship," suggesting that Cannon's long engagement with Sienese art has fed directly into a synthetic reappraisal accessible to both specialists and a wider public.

Cannon's teaching has been central to her reputation. The essays gathered in Late Medieval Italian Art and Its Contexts: Essays in Honour of Professor Joanna Cannon are all written by scholars who completed their doctorates under her supervision, indicating the scale of her influence as a doctoral supervisor. The volume's contributions span panel and wall painting, architecture, sculpture, metalwork, manuscripts and gilded glass across the thirteenth to fifteenth centuries, reflecting the range of media and topics she encourages her students to explore. Commentators note that her scholarship and teaching together have "shaped the historical study of thirteenth- and fourteenth-century Italian art," and the festschrift explicitly positions itself as a response to her "extraordinary contribution" to early Italian art studies.

Beyond her immediate students, Cannon has been active in broader scholarly networks. She has been closely associated with initiatives in medieval and Byzantine studies, and recent notices, such as a 2026 announcement by the Society for the Promotion of Byzantine Studies on the death of her husband, the art historian John Lowden, underline her position within an international community of medievalists. Through seminars, public lectures, exhibition collaborations and research networks, she has helped keep Trecento studies at the forefront of methodological innovation and interdisciplinary dialogue.

Carli, Enzo

Pisa, August 20, 1910 - Siena, September 26, 1999

Enzo Carli was one of the leading Italian art historians of the twentieth century, an authority on the study of medieval Sienese and Pisan art, as well as a museum director, curator, and extraordinarily prolific writer.

He graduated from the University of Pisa, where he was first a student of Mario Salmi, one of the founders of modern Italian medieval art history, and later of Matteo Marangoni. His thesis was dedicated to the Sienese sculptor Tino di Camaino (1285-1337), a choice that perfectly foreshadowed the two geographical and stylistic poles around which his entire research would revolve: Pisa and Siena, and the thirteenth and fourteenth centuries. Alongside his university studies, he cultivated music, studying piano and composition, and poetry with equal passion, two dimensions that accompanied his intellectual life until the end.

From 1933, he taught art history in Florentine high schools, gaining experience through direct contact with Tuscany's artistic heritage. In 1937, he was appointed Superintendent in L'Aquila, and in 1939 he was transferred to Siena, the city that became his permanent home and the center of his work for over sixty years. In Siena, he directed the Pinacoteca Nazionale until 1952 and the Museo dell'Opera Metropolitana del Duomo until 1973, two institutions that housed the masterpieces of medieval Sienese painting, from Duccio to Simone Martini, and from Pietro to Ambrogio Lorenzetti. That long tenure at the helm of Siena's museums allowed him to develop a direct, daily, and inexhaustible knowledge of the original works, which translated into a level of meticulousness in attribution and description that was rare indeed.

In 1942, he obtained a teaching qualification in Medieval and Modern Art History, which he exercised at the Faculty of Letters of the University of Pisa. In 1962, he won a university competition and was appointed to the Faculty of Letters of the University of Genoa; subsequently, he taught Art History at the University of Siena, thus combining his museum work with his academic career.

His approach to the study of artistic phenomena was always rooted in a historicism of Crocean descent: rigorous formal analysis, historical contextualization, and attention to the expressive quality of the work, without veering into formalism or sociological interpretations.

His bibliographic output was vast, over 500 titles, including monographs, specialized essays, catalogs, and popular texts. His fundamental contributions primarily concern Sienese art: I capolavori dell'arte senese (Milan, 1946), La pittura senese (Milan, 1955), and numerous monographs on individual masters, including Duccio, Simone Martini, the Lorenzetti, Giovanni di Paolo, and Sassetta. On the Pisan front, he dealt with equal depth with Romanesque sculpture, the Camposanto Monumentale, and Duecento painting. In collaboration with Gian Alberto Dell'Acqua, he edited Storia dell'arte ad uso dei licei, a textbook published in multiple volumes and editions between 1952 and 1986, which educated generations of Italian students.

In 1980, he received the President of the Republic Award as an emeritus member of the Accademia dei Lincei. In 1995, the Accademia dei Lincei awarded him the Feltrinelli Prize for Art and Poetry Criticism, one of the most prestigious honors in Italian culture, crowning a career of exceptional consistency and breadth. He died in Siena on September 26, 1999, and is buried in the Monumental Cemetery of Pisa. In his memory, his children established the Enzo Carli Fund at the University of Siena Library, which houses his books, papers, and scholarly correspondence.

Cavalcaselle, Giovanni Battista

Legnago, 22 January 1819 - Rome, 31 October 1897

Giovanni Battista Cavalcaselle was an Italian writer, critic, and art historian, born at Legnago on 22 January 1819 and dead in Rome on 31 October 1897, who is widely regarded as one of the founders of modern Italian art history and art criticism. He is especially famous for his long collaboration with Joseph Archer Crowe, with whom he produced major histories of Italian and Flemish painting that remained influential well into the twentieth century.

Cavalcaselle studied at the Accademia di Belle Arti in Venice, but from an early stage his real formation came from direct travel and close observation of artworks across Italy rather than from academic routine alone. Treccani describes him as moving from town to town on foot, visiting churches, convents, palaces, and collections, and recording newly seen works in sketches and notes that became the foundation of his later method.

In 1846-47 he traveled through Germany, especially Munich, Dresden, Leipzig, and Berlin, expanding his knowledge of northern collections and meeting the English art enthusiast Joseph Archer Crowe. When the revolutions of 1848 broke out, he returned to Italy, took part in the anti-Austrian uprising, later fought for the Roman Republic, and was eventually condemned to death in absentia by the Austrians, forcing him into exile. By 1850 he was living in London in difficult circumstances, supported only modestly by relatives and by the network of friends he had formed abroad.

In London, Cavalcaselle renewed his connection with Crowe, and together they published Early Flemish Painters in 1856, followed by the major History of Painting in Italy beginning in 1864. Their partnership later produced A History of Painting in North Italy, as well as substantial studies on Titian and Raphael, and these works helped establish a more empirical and comparative way of writing art history in English. Even though many of their individual attributions have since been revised, modern scholars still cite them because of the extraordinary breadth of firsthand looking on which their books were based.

Cavalcaselle's reputation rested above all on his powers of attribution and connoisseurship, and Italian scholarship has often treated him as a founder of modern art criticism for that reason. His sketchbooks and notes, preserved in Venice, are considered vital evidence of both his visual method and his vast range of knowledge. He also contributed to the development of restoration thinking, proposing more updated criteria at a time when the conceptual basis of restoration was still unsettled in Italy.

After Italian unification, Cavalcaselle entered public service and from 1875 to 1893 worked in Rome as a central inspector in the state administration responsible for excavations, museums, antiquities, and fine arts. In that role he traveled widely through the newly unified kingdom, advising on museum organization, acquisitions, and urgent restorations, and in some cases personally supervising interventions. For a scholar of medieval and early Renaissance art, his importance lies not only in the books he wrote, but in the union he forged between travel, archival diligence, visual analysis, attribution, and cultural administration.

Casazza, Ornella

Rezzoaglio (Genoa), 1943 – Florence, March 2020

Ornella Casazza was one of the most celebrated and respected Italian restorers of the twentieth century, widely recognized as a leading authority in the conservation of pictorial heritage. Although born in Rezzoaglio in Liguria in 1943, she spent almost her entire professional life in Florence, where she developed the career that would place her at the center of postwar restoration culture in Italy and abroad.

Her formation unfolded in a decisive historical context. Living in Florence at the time of the catastrophic flood of 4 November 1966, she experienced directly the emergency that transformed the discipline of conservation and brought her into contact with Umberto Baldini and the restoration environment that later shaped the Opificio delle Pietre Dure's post-flood methodologies. After a long workshop apprenticeship, she entered the Opificio officially in 1974, following a national competitive examination.

The range and quality of her interventions are exceptional. Her work on Cimabue's Crucifix in Santa Croce became a key site for the refinement of lacuna reintegration methods, while the restoration of Botticelli's Primavera was presented as a methodological landmark in the 1982 exhibition Method and Science. The most internationally celebrated project of her career, however, was the direction of the restoration of the Brancacci Chapel frescoes by Masaccio, Masolino, and Filippino Lippi in Santa Maria del Carmine, a campaign considered exemplary for both technical innovation and historical results. She also directed the restoration of Masaccio's San Giovenale Triptych, a fundamental work for early fifteenth-century Florentine painting.

Casazza's contribution was as theoretical as it was practical. Through sustained work on flood-damaged paintings and on major medieval and Renaissance panels, she helped define and stabilize the methods of chromatic selection and chromatic abstraction, approaches that made visual reintegration possible without falsifying the original. In this sense, her work belongs to the broader methodological transformation associated with the Florentine school of restoration in the second half of the twentieth century. Antonio Paolucci would later remember her as a restorer of exceptional standing with international recognition.

After advancing through further competitive appointments, Casazza also held major institutional roles, including leadership of advanced-technology research for conservation in Florence and direction of the Museum of Silver and Porcelain at Palazzo Pitti. She curated projects that connected restoration, decorative arts, and modern production, and remained active as a teacher and author in Italy and abroad, with publications on conservation, iconography, and painting techniques that remain widely cited. She died in Florence in March 2020. The 2021 volume L'eredità culturale di Ornella Casazza, edited by Lura Felici, stands as the principal collective tribute to her life and legacy.

Chiodo, Sonia

active late 20th-21st century

Sonia Chiodo (full name: Stella Sonia Chiodo) is one of Italy's leading historians of medieval art, specializing in Tuscan painting and miniature art from the 12th to the 15th centuries. She currently serves as Associate Professor of Medieval Art History (SSD L-ART/01) in the SAGAS Department (History, Archaeology, Geography, Art, and Performing Arts) at the University of Florence.

Chiodo graduated in 1992 from the University of Florence with a thesis on late-Gothic Florentine painting, titled The Master of the Madonna Straus. Continuities and Innovations in Florentine Painting at the Turn of the Fifteenth Century, under the guidance of the renowned art historian Mina Gregori, earning the highest honors with distinction.

In 1996, she earned a diploma from the School of Archival Science, Paleography, and Diplomatics at the State Archives of Florence, thereby refining the documentary and paleographic skills essential for research on medieval art. The following year, in 1997, she also earned a diploma with highest honors from the School of Specialization in Art History at the University of Florence.

In 2004, she earned her Ph.D. with a dissertation titled The Master of the Accademia's Misericordia. Trends in Florentine Painting of the Third Quarter of the Fourteenth Century, a work that would lay the groundwork for some of her most important monographic studies on post-plague Florentine painting. Between 1994 and 1996, she was a fellow at the prestigious Roberto Longhi Foundation for Art History Studies in Florence, a leading institution for the training of Italian art historians.

From 1999 to 2001, and then from 2004 to 2006, Chiodo was a research fellow at the University of Florence; on September 1, 2006, she became a Researcher in Medieval Art History at the same university.

On October 1, 2018, she was promoted to the position of Associate Professor of Medieval Art History, a role she still holds today. Since 2021, she has directed the Graduate School of Art Heritage at the University of Florence, further solidifying her role in training new generations of art historians.

She is head of the Corpus of Florentine Painting Laboratory within the LIEC (Interdisciplinary Laboratory on Cultural Heritage) of the SAGAS Department, and is a member of the editorial board of the journal Paragone. Arte. She served on the Executive Board of the University Council of Art Historians (CUNSTA) from 2018 to 2023.

One of the most significant scholarly achievements associated with Chiodo's name is her direction of the Corpus of Florentine Painting, the monumental cataloging project initiated by Richard Offner between 1930 and 1965, and subsequently continued by Miklos Boskovits from 1984 to 2011.

From 1997 to 2011, Chiodo actively collaborated with Boskovits on numerous volumes of the project, publishing in 2011 the volume Painters in Florence after the "Black Death": The Master of the Misericordia and Matteo di Pacino (Section IV, vol. IX), which sheds new light on little-studied aspects of Florentine painting of the late 14th century. In 2012, she took over the direction of the entire project, succeeding Boskovits, within the association chaired by Andrea De Marchi.

Starting in 2013, Chiodo designed and coordinated the creation of the Corpus's digital photo library (www.corpuspitturafiorentina.it), a pioneering initiative in the field of Digital Humanities applied to medieval art history, which made the historical archive's photographic collection accessible online.

Medieval miniatures represent one of the most fruitful areas of Chiodo's research. In 2009, she received PRIN (Project of National Interest) funding for the study of the illuminated manuscripts of Santa Croce in Florence.

In 2016, she curated the exhibition Ad usum fratris. Miniatures in the Laurentian Manuscripts of the Santa Croce Library (10th-13th Centuries), accompanied by a scholarly reference catalog. In her studies of miniatures, Chiodo pays particular attention to the relationship between text and images, the cultural context of production, and patronage, applying a rigorous method that combines philological analysis and archival research.

In 2020, she curated the exhibition Storie di pagine dipinte. Miniature recuperate dai Carabinieri at the Galleria Palatina in Florence, dedicated to ancient illuminated manuscripts stolen and recovered by the Carabinieri's Cultural Heritage Protection Unit. The exhibition, organized in collaboration with the University of Florence's School of Specialization, drew attention to the fragility of the historical and artistic heritage and the need for its protection.

As part of the 2021 Dante celebrations, Chiodo curated, together with Luca Azzetta and Teresa De Robertis, the exhibition "Honorable and Ancient Citizen of Florence": The Bargello for Dante at the Bargello National Museum, dedicated to iconographic representations of the poet.

Among her most cited contributions is the essay A Tribute to Dante: The Giottesque Portrait in the Palazzo del Podesta in Florence, published in the anthology New Horizons in Trecento Italian Art. From 2013 to 2019, she served as a member of the scientific committee of the Ente Nazionale Giovanni Boccaccio, overseeing the project Boccaccio visualizzato, and from 2019 to 2022, she served on the board of directors of the same organization.

The international dimension of her work is also evident through her affiliation with the Kunsthistorisches Institut in Florenz (KHI), the prestigious German institute for art research based in Florence, where the Corpus of Florentine Painting is headquartered.

She is regularly invited to leading international conferences, including the symposium Siena: The Rise of Painting, 1300-1350 (2025), and actively contributes to the international debate on medieval Italian painting. Her academic profile on Academia.edu has over 300 followers and 46 published works, reflecting a broad international research network.

Ciatti, Marco

Prato, 1955 – Prato, 2024

Italian art historian and one of the most influential figures in twentieth- and early twenty-first-century Italian restoration and conservation, whose career combined institutional leadership, scholarly publishing, and hands-on direction of major campaigns at the Opificio delle Pietre Dure in Florence. He graduated with honours in Literature, specialising in art history, from the University of Florence, and from 1980 served as an art historian within the Ministry of Cultural Heritage and Activities. From 1981 he worked within the Superintendence for Artistic and Historical Heritage in Siena, in the restoration team, an experience that grounded his later theoretical positions in the practical demands of monumental and panel painting conservation. In 1984 he joined the Opificio delle Pietre Dure, where he eventually became head of the restoration workshops for paintings on canvas and panel and for textile artefacts, supervising campaigns on major works by Beato Angelico, Botticelli, Caravaggio, Giotto, Raphael, Mantegna, and others — many of them damaged by the catastrophic 1966 Florence flood. From 2012 to 2022 he served as Superintendent of the Opificio, consolidating its international standing as one of Europe's foremost centres for the scientific study and technical restoration of portable paintings and textiles.

Ciatti's contribution to the scholarly literature on restoration methodology was substantial and sustained. From 1997 he edited the annual journal OPD Restauro and coordinated the Opificio's two principal publication series, Storia e teoria del restauro and Problemi di conservazione e restauro, through which he promoted a rigorous integration of conservation science, art-historical analysis, and restoration theory that distinguished the Florentine school from more purely technical or aesthetic approaches. He taught History and Theory of Restoration and History of Artistic Techniques at the universities of Florence, Siena, Bologna, and Milan, as well as at the Polytechnic, training successive generations of restorers and art historians in the interdisciplinary framework that characterised his own practice. He was elected a member of the Accademia delle Arti del Disegno and a corresponding member of the Accademia Nazionale dei Lincei, recognitions that affirmed his standing within Italian academic culture alongside his institutional achievements. He died in Prato on 20 April 2024.

Colgrave, Bertram

Derry, 1889 - Cambridge, 13 January 1968

Bertram Colgrave was one of the twentieth century's foremost authorities on the Latin hagiographic literature of early Anglo-Saxon England, best known for his critical editions of the lives of saints Cuthbert, Wilfrid, Guthlac, and Gregory the Great, and above all for his landmark edition of Bede's Ecclesiastical History of the English People.

Colgrave was born in 1889 in Derry, Ireland, and pursued a distinguished academic formation at two of England's leading universities. After completing his first degree, he went on to study Anglo-Saxon and Middle English at Clare College, Cambridge, where he developed the deep philological training in Old and Middle English that would underpin his entire scholarly career. He taught briefly at Merchiston Castle School near Edinburgh from 1916 to 1918, before moving into the full-time academic life that defined him.

Colgrave spent the central decades of his career at Durham University, and his close association with Durham was not merely institutional but deeply personal. Durham Cathedral is the resting place of Saint Cuthbert, and Colgrave wrote in the preface to his edition of the Two Lives of Saint Cuthbert that editing those texts was "almost a pious duty for one who lives under the shadow of Durham Cathedral". He became one of the university's leading scholars of early medieval hagiography, wrote the official guide to the city and diocese of Durham, and on his retirement in 1954 was celebrated as one of the institution's most distinguished figures.

His most enduring contribution to medieval studies was the preparation of scrupulously edited, bilingual Latin-English critical editions of foundational texts of early Northumbrian Christianity. His edition of the Two Lives of Saint Cuthbert, presenting both the anonymous Life composed by a monk of Lindisfarne between 698 and 705 and Bede's polished prose Life of 721, remains a standard scholarly reference. Taken together, the two texts illuminate the golden age of Northumbrian monasticism and the ecclesiastical tensions between Celtic and Roman practice, resolved in the generation after Whitby.

He also prepared editions of the Latin Life of Wilfrid by Eddius Stephanus, the Life of Guthlac by Felix, and the earliest Life of Gregory the Great by the anonymous monk of Whitby, works that form the core corpus of early Anglo-Saxon hagiography. His greatest posthumous achievement was the monumental critical edition of Bede's Ecclesiastical History, co-edited with R. A. B. Mynors and published by Oxford University Press in 1969, the year after his death. In the Oxford Medieval Texts series, with facing Latin text and Colgrave's English translation, it has remained continuously in print and is cited in virtually all anglophone scholarship on early medieval England.

Beyond editorial work, Colgrave served from 1950 to 1963 as the founding editor-in-chief of Early English Manuscripts in Facsimile, a landmark series that made major Anglo-Saxon manuscripts accessible to scholars internationally. This role placed him at the intersection of palaeography, codicology, and literary history, the same intersection in which illuminated manuscript studies are grounded. After retiring from Durham in 1954, he held visiting professorships at the University of North Carolina, the University of Texas, the University of Kansas, the University of Colorado, and Mount Holyoke College, carrying the study of Bede and early English hagiography to American audiences.

Colgrave died on 13 January 1968 in Cambridge. His editions remain indispensable tools for scholars working on early medieval England, Northumbrian Christianity, and the Latin literary culture of the seventh and eighth centuries, a body of work whose care, erudition, and longevity place him among the major medievalists of the twentieth century.

Coletti, Luigi

Treviso, 1886 – Treviso, 1961

Luigi Coletti was one of the most important Italian art historians of the twentieth century, specializing in medieval and Venetian art, with a particular focus on fourteenth-century painters and the so-called Italian primitives. His work became a central reference for scholarship on Italian painting between the Romanesque and Gothic periods.

Born in Treviso to a family from Cadore with a long-standing local tradition, Coletti entered the city's intellectual world early, coming into contact during his school years with figures such as Luigi Bailo and Augusto Serena. His academic formation followed an unusual path: he completed a law degree at the University of Padua in 1909, while already publishing studies on local art. Friendships formed after the First World War, especially with Giuseppe Fiocco and later Carlo Gamba Ghiselli, decisively shaped his methodological and critical orientation.

In 1919, Coletti was appointed chair of the commission for the reorganization of the Treviso Civic Museum, and in 1932 he obtained a lectureship in art history, succeeding Abbot Bailo as curator of the Museum and Civic Art Gallery. His curatorial activity was both scholarly and operational: he led the reordering of civic collections, promoted conservation campaigns for the walls of Fra Giocondo and the Loggia dei Cavalieri, and initiated restoration work at San Francesco. He also acquired the Bampo manuscript on local art for the Municipal Library and founded the Museo della Casa Trevigiana in Ca' de Noal.

The high point of his scholarship was the trilogy on the Italian Primitives: Dal romanico a Giotto (1941), I senesi e i giotteschi (1946), and I padani (1947). Together these volumes offered a systematic account of medieval Italian painting from Romanesque beginnings to the Gothic developments of the mid-fourteenth century, combining stylistic analysis, regional comparison, and historical framing of the Sienese, Florentine, and Padanian schools.

In 1946, Coletti accepted a university chair in Trieste, deliberately choosing the city during a politically sensitive phase to contribute to its cultural and institutional consolidation within the Italian sphere. Elected dean, he is remembered for balancing academic leadership with a commitment to ideological pluralism in teaching. During the Second World War he had already played a key role in the protection of Treviso's artistic patrimony, coordinating the evacuation and safeguarding of artworks between 1940 and 1943.

Author of more than 150 publications, including articles, essays, and monographs, Coletti worked across medieval and Venetian Renaissance art, with important studies on Lorenzo Lotto, Tommaso da Modena, Veronese, and Canova. He served on the editorial board of Arte Veneta, on the board of the Institute of Art History of the Cini Foundation, and as chairman of the board of trustees of the Trieste Museum. Retiring from university teaching in 1957, he remained active in Treviso, continuing to supervise the final reorganization of the Civic Museums until his death.

Constable, Giles

London, 1 June 1929 – Princeton, 17 January 2021

Giles Constable FBA, born on 1 June 1929 in London, was one of the most distinguished and influential medieval historians of the twentieth century, whose prolific scholarship fundamentally reshaped the study of religious and intellectual life in the eleventh and twelfth centuries. He died on 17 January 2021 in Princeton, New Jersey, at the age of 91.

Constable came from a family already distinguished in the world of art and scholarship: his father, William George Constable, was a noted art historian, and his mother was Olivia Roberts. This cultivated background nurtured in him a lifelong sensitivity to the interconnections between religious culture, material art, and intellectual history — a sensibility that would prove enormously productive throughout his career. He received his undergraduate degree (A.B.) from Harvard University in 1950, spent 1952–53 at Cambridge, and returned to Harvard to complete his Ph.D. in 1957.

Constable's teaching career began at the University of Iowa (1955–58), before he joined the faculty of Harvard University in 1958, where he remained for over two decades. At Harvard he rose to hold the prestigious Henry Charles Lea Professorship of Medieval History from 1966 to 1977 — the oldest and most distinguished chair in medieval studies at an American university. His Harvard years were enormously productive, establishing him as the preeminent American authority on the religious history of the central Middle Ages.

From 1977 to 1984 he served as Director of Dumbarton Oaks Research Library and Collection in Washington D.C., the Harvard-affiliated research institute devoted to Byzantine, pre-Columbian, and garden studies. This role gave him both administrative authority and the resources to pursue wide-ranging collaborative scholarship, and it deepened his already considerable familiarity with Byzantine history and culture. In 1985 he joined the permanent faculty of the Institute for Advanced Study in Princeton — one of the world's most celebrated centers of pure research — as Professor in the School of Historical Studies, a post he held until becoming Professor Emeritus in 2003.

The animating center of Constable's scholarship was the religious and cultural history of the eleventh and twelfth centuries, a period he regarded as one of the most creative and transformative in the entire history of Western Christianity. He was particularly fascinated by the Abbey of Cluny and its great abbot Peter the Venerable (c. 1092–1156), whose intellectual and spiritual world he explored with a depth and precision that no previous scholar had matched. Cluny, as the head of the largest monastic network in medieval Europe, represented for Constable the fullest expression of the Benedictine ideal — and also its most productive tensions with the newer religious movements of the age.

His most celebrated single work is The Reformation of the Twelfth Century (1996), a sweeping and erudite synthesis that interpreted the explosion of new religious orders, hermit movements, and spiritual experiments in the 1100s as a coherent reforming impulse comparable in scope to the Protestant Reformation of the sixteenth century. The book became a standard reference for all students of medieval religious history. His two-volume critical edition of Peter the Venerable's letters, published by Harvard University Press in 1967, remains the authoritative text. Together with Robert Benson he edited the landmark collaborative volume Renaissance and Renewal in the Twelfth Century (1982), a collection that brought together the finest medievalists of the era and consolidated the scholarly argument for a genuine "twelfth-century renaissance".

His scholarly range extended well beyond monasticism. Together with Alexander Kazhdan he co-authored People and Power in Byzantium (1982), an introduction to Byzantine studies that opened a productive dialogue between Western medieval history and the Byzantine tradition. He also wrote authoritatively on the Crusades, on the history of monastic tithes, on medieval attitudes to wealth, poverty, and social order, and on the methodological and historiographical challenges of medieval studies itself.

Constable devoted enormous energy to the infrastructure of the discipline. For twenty years, from 1958 to 1978, he served as Assistant Editor of Speculum, the flagship journal of the Medieval Academy of America — work that shaped the intellectual standards of the field for a generation. He sat on the editorial boards of the Journal of Ecclesiastical History, Medievalia et Humanistica, and the Revue Mabillon, among others. He also served on the scientific council of the Revue d'Histoire Ecclésiastique.

As a visiting professor he lectured at Saint John's University in Collegeville, the Catholic University of America, Georgetown University, Princeton University, and Arizona State University — bringing his scholarship to a wide range of academic communities.

The breadth and depth of Constable's contributions were recognized by learned societies across the world. He was elected a Fellow of the Medieval Academy of America, the American Historical Association, the American Philosophical Society, the British Academy (FBA), the Royal Historical Society, the Académie des Inscriptions et Belles-Lettres in Paris, the Bavarian Academy of Sciences, and two of Italy's most prestigious scholarly institutions: the Instituto Lombardo and the Accademia Nazionale dei Lincei. Honorary doctorates were conferred upon him by the University of Paris I, Georgetown University, Longwood University, and the Pontifical Institute of Medieval Studies in Toronto.

Giles Constable's legacy rests on his extraordinary capacity to combine meticulous archival and textual scholarship with broad synthetic vision. He did not merely edit documents or compile facts: he asked large questions about the nature of medieval religious experience, the meaning of monastic reform, and the relationship between institutional structures and individual spirituality, and he answered those questions with lucidity and authority. For scholars engaged in the study of medieval manuscript culture, religious iconography, and the ecclesiastical world of the twelfth and thirteenth centuries, Constable's work remains an indispensable intellectual companion — a guide to the spiritual and cultural landscape within which the great artists and illuminators of the medieval centuries worked and believed.

Cormack, Robin Sinclair

b. 27 September 1938

Robin Sinclair Cormack, FSA (born 27 September 1938) is one of Britain's foremost classicists and art historians, internationally recognized for his groundbreaking scholarship in Byzantine art, icon studies, and the cultural history of the Eastern Christian world. His career, spanning more than five decades, placed him at the heart of the Courtauld Institute of Art and cemented his reputation as a leading authority on the visual culture of Byzantium.

Cormack received his secondary education at Bristol Grammar School before reading classics at Exeter College, Oxford. He then pursued graduate study at the Courtauld Institute of Art, University of London, where he completed his doctoral dissertation on the art of Thessaloniki in the aftermath of iconoclasm. The dissertation was supervised by two giants of Byzantine studies, Hugo Buchthal and Cyril Mango, and it was Mango who first encouraged Cormack to extend his research at Dumbarton Oaks, the pre-eminent research center for Byzantine studies in Washington, D.C.

Cormack joined the Courtauld Institute as a lecturer in 1966, a position he held until 1982. During this period he took a formative leave of absence in the academic year 1972-73, when he became a visiting fellow in Byzantine studies at Dumbarton Oaks, an experience that profoundly shaped his research trajectory. He subsequently spent three years as a reader at the Warburg Institute, another prestigious center for cultural and classical studies, and also held a fellowship at Robinson College, Cambridge between 1984 and 1985.

He returned to the Courtauld as reader and then professor, eventually being appointed to a full Chair in the History of Art in 1991, a position he held until his retirement in 2004. During his tenure, he also served as the Institute's deputy director from 1999 to 2002. His photographs documenting architectural monuments are preserved in the Courtauld's Conway Library, currently being digitized as part of the broader Courtauld Connects project.

Rather than retreating from academic life, Cormack's retirement in 2004 opened a new phase of active scholarship. He was awarded a Leverhulme Emeritus Fellowship (2004-2006) and a scholarship at the Getty Research Institute in Los Angeles (2005-06), followed by an appointment as Special Professor in Classics at the University of Nottingham (2005-2008). He returned to Dumbarton Oaks once more as a visiting scholar in 2011. Since then, he has been an invited lecturer in the Faculty of Classics at the University of Cambridge, where his wife, the classicist Mary Beard, is Professor of Classics, and holds the title of Professor Emeritus in the History of Art at the University of London, as well as Senior Academic Visitor at Wolfson College, Cambridge.

His ongoing research centers on the cultural history of Saint Catherine's Monastery on the Sinai Peninsula, one of the oldest and most important repositories of Byzantine icons and manuscripts in the world, tracing its significance from Late Antiquity to the present.

Beyond his scholarly writing, Cormack made a significant contribution to the public understanding of Byzantine art through his curatorial work. His interest in exhibitions began in his student years, when he worked at the Institute of Contemporary Arts (ICA) under Roland Penrose and Herbert Read. His first independent exhibition featured Bulgarian icons in Edinburgh in the 1970s. He later served as a consultant to the Royal Academy of Arts, London for the landmark exhibitions From Byzantine to El Greco (1987) and The Art of Holy Russia: Icons from Moscow 1400-1660 (1998). His most celebrated curatorial achievement was co-curating, with Professor Maria Vassilaki of the University of Thessaly and the Benaki Museum, the Royal Academy's monumental exhibition Byzantium 330-1453 (2008-2009), which brought together iconic works from across the former Byzantine world.

Cormack's bibliography reflects a career dedicated to making Byzantine art accessible to both scholars and general readers. His debut monograph, Writing in Gold: Byzantine Society and its Icons (Oxford University Press, 1985), set a new standard for contextualizing icons within Byzantine social and religious life and was translated into French. Painting the Soul: Icons, Death Masks, and Shrouds (Reaktion Books, 1997) won the Runciman Award in 1998. His volume Byzantine Art (Oxford University Press, 2000), part of the Oxford History of Art series, became an essential introductory text for students of the field.

He also authored Icons (British Museum Press, 2007) and co-edited the Oxford Handbook of Byzantine Studies (2008) with Elizabeth Jeffreys and John Haldon. Together, these works constitute a body of scholarship that has shaped how Byzantine art is studied, taught, and appreciated internationally.

Cormack married Annabel Shackleton in 1961; they had a daughter, Sophia, and a son, Justin. After the dissolution of that marriage, he married the classicist and public intellectual Mary Beard in 1985; together they have a daughter, Zoe, and a son, Raphael Cormack (born 1987), who has himself become a scholar and translator. Cormack is a Fellow of the Society of Antiquaries of London (FSA).

Crowe, Joseph Archer

London, 25 October 1825 – Gamburg an der Tauber, 6 September 1896

Joseph Archer Crowe was an English journalist, diplomat, and art historian whose work with Giovanni Battista Cavalcaselle helped give art history in English a more rigorous, evidence-based method. Born in London on 25 October 1825 and dead at Gamburg an der Tauber, in present-day Germany, on 6 September 1896, he belonged to that rare nineteenth-century generation whose careers moved easily between politics, letters, and scholarship.

Crowe was born into a cultivated and cosmopolitan family as the son of the journalist Eyre Evans Crowe, and he spent much of his boyhood in France, especially in Paris, where his father worked as a correspondent and where the family home became a meeting place for liberal and artistic circles. His education was directed largely by his father, and this early exposure to languages, journalism, and continental culture gave him an unusually international outlook for an English writer of his time. He was also the brother of the painter Eyre Crowe, so the visual arts were part of his family world from the beginning.

After returning to England in the 1840s, Crowe entered journalism, working for the Morning Chronicle and the Daily News before serving as a correspondent for the Illustrated London News during the Crimean War. He later reported for The Times during the Indian Mutiny and the Austro-Italian War, and he was present at the Battle of Solferino, an experience that placed him close to one of the defining military events of nineteenth-century Europe. This journalistic training sharpened the habits of close observation and factual scrutiny that later marked his writing on painting and attribution.

In 1860 Crowe entered the British diplomatic service as consul-general for Saxony at Leipzig, beginning a second career that ran alongside his scholarly work for decades. He later served at Dusseldorf, then as commercial attache in Berlin and Vienna, and eventually held responsibility for commercial affairs across Europe; he also represented Britain at the Danube Conference in 1883, the Samoa Conference in 1889, and the Telegraph Congress in Paris in 1890. His public service was recognized with appointment as Companion of the Bath in 1885 and Knight Commander of the Order of St. Michael and St. George in 1890, and his private life included a marriage in 1861 to Asta von Barby, with whom he had a large family.

Crowe's lasting fame rests above all on his partnership with Giovanni Battista Cavalcaselle, the Italian critic and connoisseur with whom he collaborated on a series of major works that transformed the study of European painting. Their first major book, The Early Flemish Painters of 1856, was followed by A New History of Painting in Italy from the Second to the Sixteenth Century and then by A History of Painting in North Italy, as well as substantial monographs on Titian and Raphael. In these works, Crowe supplied much of the prose structure and literary form, while the collaboration as a whole depended on sustained travel, firsthand viewing, stylistic comparison, and the attempt to reconstruct the historical development of individual artists.

What made Crowe important was not simply the number of books he wrote, but the method those books embodied: a disciplined reliance on direct visual examination, chronology, and connoisseurship rather than on inherited tradition alone. Even though many individual attributions made by Crowe and Cavalcaselle have been revised by later scholars, their work still stands near the beginning of modern art-historical writing in English because it treated painting as something to be studied critically, comparatively, and historically. Crowe published his memoirs, Reminiscences of Thirty-Five Years of My Life, in 1895, one year before his death, leaving behind the image of a Victorian scholar whose authority was built as much on travel and observation as on reading and theory.

Cuppini, Luciano

active second half of the 20th century

Luciano Cuppini was an Italian art historian active in Verona during the second half of the twentieth century, serving as a key link between the great tradition of Longhian criticism and local research on Venetian medieval and Renaissance art. Although he never held a university chair, his influence was exerted with extraordinary effectiveness through his teaching and scholarly contributions, leaving a lasting mark on Veronese art historiography.

Cuppini trained as a direct student of Roberto Longhi, the greatest Italian art historian of the twentieth century, known for his method based on close examination of works and a stylistic analysis of extraordinary acuity. This apprenticeship left an indelible mark on Cuppini's method: his ability to interpret pictorial and sculptural forms, to identify individual artists and workshops, and to link archival documents to anonymous artistic figures directly reflects the Longhi school. The correspondence preserved at the Longhi Foundation in Florence documents a letter-writing relationship between the two dating back at least to 1950, attesting to an intellectual association that continued even after their formative years.

Upon returning to Verona, Cuppini began a career as a teacher of art history at the Liceo Classico Scipione Maffei, one of the city's most prestigious schools, historically linked to Verona's antiquarian and literary culture. His teaching was instrumental in shaping a generation of art historians: among his students was Paola Marini, who would later direct the Civic Art Museums of Verona from 1997 to 2015 and subsequently the Gallerie dell'Accademia in Venice until 2018. Cuppini was thus not only a researcher but also an educator capable of transmitting the rigor of the attribution method to future professionals in Italian cultural heritage.

On the scholarly level, Cuppini focused on two major thematic areas. The first concerns medieval painting and sculpture in Verona: together with Maria Teresa Cuppini, presumably a close collaborator or family member, he developed in the 1950s and 1960s the critical identification of the sculptural corpus of Giovanni di Rigino, the leading fourteenth-century Veronese sculptor and creator of signed works, thereby contributing to defining the artistic character of Verona under the Scaliger dynasty. His studies on the Arca di Cangrande I della Scala proposed specific attributions for the equestrian statues and funerary reliefs that subsequent critics would debate and, in part, revise.

The second area concerns thirteenth-century Italian painting in the broadest sense. In 1952, he published the essay Ranieri di Ugolino (Commentari, vol. 3, pp. 7-13), in which he identified the Pisan painter Ranieri di Ugolino with the so-called Master of San Martino, the creator of the large panel preserved at the National Museum of San Matteo in Pisa. This proposal, shared by Garrison and supported by Battisti in 1963, did not gain definitive acceptance from subsequent critics, but it attests to the breadth of Cuppini's interests, which ranged from local Veronese painting to thirteenth-century Pisan panel paintings.

A little-known but significant aspect of Cuppini's life is his collecting. In 1993, he donated to the Castelvecchio Museum in Verona a substantial private collection of ancient drawings, which remains a valuable documentary resource for the study of Venetian graphic art. This donation, likely made shortly before his death, was an act of returning to the Veronese community the heritage he had accumulated over decades of study and research.

Luciano Cuppini's legacy can be measured on two distinct yet complementary levels. As a researcher, his contributions to our understanding of Scaliger sculpture and thirteenth-century Pisan painting remain key references in the scholarly literature, cited in major studies on medieval art in Verona and Tuscany. As a teacher and mentor, his influence lives on in the careers of his students and in the direction of art studies in Verona during the second half of the twentieth century, at a time when the city was establishing its own museums and cultural institutions. He epitomizes the figure of the high-profile local scholar who, despite not holding a university chair, worked with a methodological rigor fully comparable to that of his academic colleagues.

D

De Angelis, Luigi

Citta della Pieve, 1759 - Siena, 13 August 1832

Luigi De Angelis was one of the most significant figures in Sienese culture at the turn of the eighteenth and nineteenth centuries: theologian, librarian, scholar, and art historian, he played a decisive role in preserving Siena's artistic and literary heritage during the Napoleonic period.

Born in 1759 in Citta della Pieve (Perugia), where his father held a papal office, he moved with his family in 1764 to Piancastagnaio, in the Sienese area. Orphaned young, he found support with the Preti Ricci family of Abbadia San Salvatore. On 17 February 1775, still a teenager, he entered the convent of San Francesco in Siena as a novice, beginning a solid Franciscan ecclesiastical career.

After advanced theological studies, he earned a degree in Dogmatic Theology at the College of Saint Bonaventure in Rome in 1786. From 1791 he taught Philosophy and Moral Theology in the seminaries of Foligno and Florence, and in 1792 returned to Siena as custodian of the Franciscan custody.

His ascent in the Franciscan hierarchy was rapid: guardian of the monastery in 1798, examiner of clergy for the Diocese of Siena in 1801, and definitor for the Province of Tuscia in 1802. His scholarly prestige and ties with ruling elites led to his appointment, on 14 April 1803, to the chair of Dogmatic Theology at the University of Siena by Maria Luisa of Bourbon, regent queen of the Kingdom of Etruria. In 1805 he was also charged with reviewing librettos for operas and theatrical performances in Siena.

When the French administration abolished the University of Siena in 1808, De Angelis lost his chair but reoriented his career with remarkable diplomatic skill. On 11 March 1810 he was appointed librarian of the Public Library of Siena, reopened on 4 April 1810 as an institution of the Civic Community. In that office he transformed the library into a major center for collecting manuscripts, incunabula, and artworks from Napoleonic suppressions, expanding holdings from roughly ten thousand to more than forty-five thousand volumes.

His impact on art preservation was equally decisive. Paintings gathered from suppressed religious institutions, together with earlier collections assembled by Giuseppe Ciaccheri, formed the founding nucleus of what became the National Art Gallery of Siena. De Angelis can therefore be regarded as the effective founder of Siena's first public art museum.

With the reopening of the University in 1814, he resumed teaching theology while also serving as curator of the Cabinet of Fine Arts. In Sienese art historiography, he continued the municipal scholarly tradition associated with figures such as Giulio Mancini and Guglielmo Della Valle, promoting the reevaluation of Sienese painting against the dominant Florentine model. He devoted special attention to Fra Giacomo da Torrita (Jacopo Torriti), authoring Notizie istorico-critiche and praising him as a master of the renewal of Italian mosaic art and a precursor to Simone Martini.

He was also the first scholar to recognize the historical and artistic value of the Biccherna Panels, at a time when they were still largely neglected by critics.

Among his principal publications are Discorso storico su l'Università di Siena (Siena, 1810), Ragguaglio del nuovo Istituto delle Belle Arti stabilito in Siena con la descrizione della sala nella quale sono distribuiti cronologicamente i quadri dell'antica Scuola Senese (Siena, 1816), and Capitoli dei Disciplinati della venerabile Compagnia della Madonna sotto le volte dell'Imperiale e Reale Spedale di Santa Maria della Scala di Siena (Siena, 1818), which includes on pages 153-282 an important catalogue of Italian-language manuscripts in the Siena Municipal Library that remains a key reference for researchers. He also left an unpublished manuscript, Di Giulio Mancini elogio, later published and studied as evidence of his commitment to revaluing Sienese art history.

In 1825 he was appointed provost of the collegiate church of Santa Maria in Provenzano in Siena. The following year he purchased, with his own funds, the ruined chapel of San Francesco all'Alberino, restored it, and dedicated to it the monograph Dell'albero di San Francesco vicino alle mura di Siena (Siena, 1827). He died in Siena on 13 August 1832 and was buried in that same chapel. His biography has been comprehensively reconstructed by Alessandro Leoncini in Luigi De Angelis. A Life Between University, Library, and Art Gallery (University of Siena, 2014), presented at the Biblioteca degli Intronati in March 2015.

De Francovich, Géza

Gorizia, 1902 – Rome, 1996

Géza de Francovich was one of the most wide-ranging and methodologically rigorous Italian art historians of the twentieth century, whose scholarly interests spanned from Romanesque sculpture to the art of the ancient Near East.

De Francovich was born on August 28, 1902, in Gorizia — then part of the Austro-Hungarian Empire — into an aristocratic family of Hungarian origin. He attended the German-language section of the Staatsgymnasium in Gorizia before the First World War disrupted his youth. After the conflict, he moved to Florence, where he completed his university degree in 1925. His formation thus combined Central European cultural sensibility with a deep immersion in the Italian art historical tradition, an unusual and productive duality that would shape his entire career.

Following his graduation, de Francovich took up a post as inspector at the Soprintendenza of Perugia from 1926 to 1928. He was subsequently recruited by the philosopher Giovanni Gentile to serve as a contributor to the Enciclopedia Italiana, where he worked alongside some of the leading art historians of the time — Pietro Toesca, Lionello Venturi, and Mario Salmi — whose assistant he also became at the University of Rome. He obtained his libera docenza (the Italian qualification for independent university teaching) from 1937, and from 1956 to 1972 he held the chair of medieval art history at the Università degli Studi di Roma "La Sapienza".

De Francovich's research was characterized by a rigorous historical method that placed artistic objects firmly within their broader ideological and cultural contexts. His thematic range was exceptional: he moved from Lombard Carolingian and Ottonian art to Romanesque wood sculpture, from Byzantine ivory carving to Achaemenid and Sasanian art, always grounding his analysis in direct examination of the monuments themselves.
