Among his foreign scholarly counterparts were Émile Bertaux—author of the seminal L'art dans l'Italie méridionale (1903)—and G. Rohault de Fleury, with whom he collaborated on studies of medieval goldsmithing, fostering dialogue between Abruzzo research and French medieval studies.

The most emblematic moment in Piccirilli's life was his response to the Marsica earthquake of January 13, 1915, which completely destroyed Avezzano and devastated dozens of towns in western Abruzzo, burying sculptures, frescoes, liturgical furnishings, and archives of immense value under the rubble. Piccirilli, by then sixty-five years old, rushed to the affected areas and remained there for weeks, working in conditions of extreme cold and hardship, leading the recovery of salvageable works with an energy and determination that Superintendent Carlo Ignazio Gavini, in his seminal History of Architecture in Abruzzo (1927), recalled as one of the most shining examples of dedication to artistic heritage. His son Guido Piccirilli (1885–1968) continued his father's research, becoming himself a scholar of Abruzzo art history. Pietro Piccirilli passed away on March 8, 1921, leaving behind a bibliographic output of over fifty-five contributions published between 1882 and 1919 in journals such as Venturi's L'Arte, Napoli Nobilissima, the Rassegna Abruzzese, and the Rassegna d'Arte.

Post, Chandler Rathfon

Detroit, 1881 – Foxborough, 1959

American art historian renowned for his expertise in Spanish and Italian Renaissance art, particularly iconography and painting traditions. Born on December 14, 1881, in Detroit, Michigan, Post pursued his education at Harvard University, earning a BA in Spanish literature in 1904. He later studied Greek literature at the American School of Classical Studies in Athens and returned to Harvard for a PhD in 1909, with a thesis on fifteenth-century Castilian allegory influenced by Dante. Post joined Harvard's faculty in 1914 as assistant professor of Greek art and fine arts, advancing to the William Dorr Boardman Professor of Fine Arts by 1934 and teaching until 1950, when he became professor emeritus. He served as an interpreter for the American Red Cross Commission to Italy during the First World War, blending his linguistic skills with art historical pursuits.

Post's scholarship focused on comprehensive art surveys, beginning with the two-volume History of European and American Sculpture (1921), one of the earliest such works produced in America. His magnum opus, the fourteen-volume A History of Spanish Painting (1930–1953), meticulously documented Spanish art from the medieval to the Renaissance period, with the final volumes completed posthumously by Harold Edwin Wethey. This series remains a foundational reference for understanding regional styles, attributions, and iconographic developments in Iberian painting, and provides valuable context for transitional periods relevant to twelfth- to fourteenth-century iconography.

A member of the American Academy of Arts and Sciences and the American Philosophical Society, Post's rigorous methodologies advanced connoisseurship and stylistic analysis in Renaissance studies. Among his notable students were the writer John Dos Passos and the art historian Walter William Spencer Cook, reflecting his influence on future scholars. He died on November 2, 1959, in Foxborough, Massachusetts.

Procacci, Ugo

Florence, 31 March 1905 – Florence, 19 February 1991

Ugo Procacci was born in Florence on 31 March 1905 into a lower-middle-class family; his father Virgilio worked as a railway inspector and his mother Amelia Faini was a housewife. He studied at the University of Florence, where he graduated in 1927 with a thesis in art history, coming into contact with the environment of the Rivista d'Arte and with the Florentine school of historians of medieval and Renaissance art. After a short period as an assistant at the university, he entered the Soprintendenza alle Gallerie of Florence at the end of the 1920s, beginning a career as a state official that would last more than four decades. In 1932 he became director of the newly founded Gabinetto dei Restauri of the Florentine Soprintendenza, a laboratory that he built up into a crucial centre for the study and restoration of panel painting.

During the 1930s Procacci combined his work as a civil servant with an intense scholarly activity, publishing in the Rivista d'Arte and other journals studies on Tuscan painting between the fourteenth and seventeenth centuries. He participated in the organization of major exhibitions such as the 1933 Tesoro di Firenze sacra and the 1937 Mostra giottesca, for which he carried out systematic surveys of churches, pievi and minor collections in the Florentine countryside, including the Mugello. These campaigns led to the identification and "rediscovery" of many neglected medieval and early Renaissance works, which Procacci studied and often re-attributed on the basis of stylistic and documentary analysis.

From an early stage, Procacci was recognized as a specialist in medieval and early Renaissance painting, particularly in the Tuscan and Florentine context. His research addressed both major figures — Giotto, Masaccio, Vasari — and lesser-known masters, insisting on the importance of documentary evidence and on the study of the "life" of works of art, including their later alterations and restorations. At the same time, he developed an innovative conception of restoration, which he helped to ground on scientific, technical and historical bases through the work of the Florentine restoration laboratory.

Under his direction, the Gabinetto dei Restauri became a pioneering institution in which art historians, chemists, photographers and restorers collaborated closely. Among the best-known episodes of his technical and connoisseurship acumen is the discovery, during investigations on Masaccio's Trinità in Santa Maria Novella, of the painted skeleton underlying the lower part of the fresco, a memento mori long hidden by later interventions. In other cases, like the cleaning of the Madonna with Child from San Giovanni Battista a Remole, the laboratory under Procacci experimented with mechanical cleaning techniques using a scalpel, inaugurating practices that would become widely discussed in the theory of restoration.

His approach to restoration sought to "return to the work its authentic image" through the removal of repaintings and accretions, but also insisted on respect for the material history of the object and on prudence in reconstruction, often opting for neutral reintegration and reversible solutions. Because of these contributions, later accounts describe him as one of the founders of modern scientific restoration in Italy and as a fundamental reference point for debates on conservation ethics.

Procacci's career as a civil servant and historian intersected dramatically with the events of the Second World War and the flood of 1966. During the war he was among the Italian officials who organized the transfer and protection of artworks from Florence and Tuscany, actions that later earned him recognition as a kind of civilian "monuments man". After the devastating flood of the Arno in November 1966, by then Soprintendente of Florence, he was among the first to rush to Santa Croce, where Cimabue's great Crocifisso and many other works had been gravely damaged.

In his report to the Ministry of Education of 30 November 1966, Procacci described the destruction as worse than any "più infausta previsione" and recounted how, in the absence of fire brigade support, the staff of the Soprintendenza and the restorers had to work alone in the mud to dismantle and secure the crucifix. He took the crucial decision, later considered decisive, to carry out all restoration work in Florence, despite difficulties and international offers of help from laboratories abroad. This choice ensured homogeneity of methods, central coordination of operations and the creation, in Florence, of an extraordinary experimental hub that accelerated the development of restoration techniques and research.

In the decades after the flood, Procacci continued to oversee restoration campaigns and to publish on the results, helping to shape both the practical and theoretical framework within which Italian conservation was reinvented. His role as Soprintendente and as a public intellectual made him a central figure in discussions on the relationship between modernization, urban planning and the protection of historic centres, especially in Florence.

Alongside his activity as official and restorer, Ugo Procacci was also a prolific scholar and editor. He published numerous studies on Florentine and Tuscan art from the Trecento to the Seicento, many of them in the Rivista d'Arte, a journal with which his career was closely intertwined and which he eventually directed. His writings combined connoisseurship, archival research and technical observation, often reattributing works, clarifying workshop practices and reconstructing the historical contexts of commissions.

Even after his retirement from the Soprintendenza in 1970, Procacci continued to work as a professor at the University of Florence, where he transmitted his integrated vision of art history — concerned with the material, social and institutional life of artworks — to new generations of scholars. Colleagues and pupils later described him as a master of a history of art attentive to "la vita degli artisti e delle opere in tutti i loro aspetti", from creation to conservation and reception. His essays were later collected and re-read in volumes such as Tra Giotto e Vasari, which emphasize his contribution to understanding the long continuity of Florentine artistic traditions.

Recognition of his importance continued after his death: in 2006 the library of the Opificio delle Pietre Dure in Florence was named "Biblioteca Ugo Procacci" in honour of his role in founding the first modern restoration laboratory in Italy. A prize for young scholars in the field of art history and conservation, the "Premio Ugo Procacci", further testifies to his lasting prestige and to the desire to link his name to innovation in research.

Ugo Procacci died in Florence on 19 February 1991. By then he was widely regarded as one of the foremost Italian art historians of the twentieth century, particularly in the field of medieval and early Renaissance painting, and as a founding father of scientific conservation in Italy. His connoisseurial discoveries contributed to the re-evaluation and "rediscovery" of many medieval works and contexts, both in the great museums and in minor ecclesiastical settings.

Perhaps even more important is the model he offered of an art historian who is simultaneously scholar, civil servant and custodian of a city's heritage, actively engaged in protecting and rescuing works in times of crisis. Thanks to his decisions during the flood and to his long work at the Gabinetto dei Restauri, Florence became an international centre of excellence for restoration, attracting expertise and resources from around the world. For these reasons, recent commemorations in Florence and in the scholarly world continue to present him as a "custode di Firenze" and as a reference figure for those working at the intersection of medieval art history and conservation.

R

Ragghianti, Carlo Ludovico

Lucca, 1910 – Florence, 1987

Carlo Ludovico Ragghianti (Lucca, March 18, 1910 - Florence, August 3, 1987) was one of the leading Italian art historians, critics, and theorists of the twentieth century. His career united rigorous methodological reflection with an exceptional level of civic and political engagement, making him a central figure in modern Italian intellectual life.

He received his early formation in Lucca and developed a precocious anti-fascist stance by the mid-1930s. He then moved to Pisa, studying art history at the Scuola Normale Superiore and the University of Pisa in an environment of high critical discipline. Giovanni Gentile, despite deep ideological differences, recognized Ragghianti's intellectual gifts and supported him at the start of his career. In 1935, together with Ranuccio Bianchi Bandinelli and with Gentile's backing, he founded Critica d'Arte, a journal that also involved Roberto Longhi and became one of the key instruments for methodological renewal in postwar Italian art history.

Ragghianti's most original contribution was his formulation of a rigorous aesthetic historicism, grounded in both formal analysis and historical interpretation. He rejected both pure formalism and reductive social-documentary readings of art, seeking instead to reconstruct the internal development of visual language over time. In contrast to approaches centered on stylistic rupture, he emphasized continuity and progression, a perspective that shaped his reevaluation of neglected periods, including nineteenth-century Italian art, and also informed his long-range inquiry into prehistoric visual culture, culminating in L'uomo cosciente, arte e conoscenza nella paleostoria (1981).

His biography was inseparable from political action. During the Resistance he became one of the main leaders in Tuscany, heading the Actionist Military Committee, founding and commanding the Rosselli Brigades until June 1944, and then presiding over the Tuscan National Liberation Committee. In this role he directed the provisional government that led the uprising against Nazi-Fascist forces in Florence, liberated on August 11, 1944. In 1945 he served as Undersecretary of Education in the Parri government, with responsibility for Fine Arts and Performing Arts, promoting urgent measures for postwar cultural reconstruction.

His institutional work remained marked by a reformist vision. In 1957 he founded the Cabinet of Drawings and Prints at the University of Pisa, developing it into both a major twentieth-century graphic collection and a center for research and exhibitions. In 1964 he founded and directed the Italian Society of Archaeology and Art History to strengthen collaboration between university and heritage administration. In 1967 he curated the landmark exhibition Arte moderna in Italia 1915-1935 at Palazzo Strozzi, a major application of his historical-critical method to twentieth-century art.

Ragghianti's legacy is preserved through the Fondazione Centro Studi sull'Arte Licia e Carlo Ludovico Ragghianti in Lucca, established according to his and his wife Licia's wishes as a permanent institution for research, exhibitions, and cultural dissemination. His archival papers, conserved in the Tuscan State Archives, remain a primary source for the history of twentieth-century Italian art criticism. He continues to be an essential reference point for scholars studying the relationship between artistic interpretation, public responsibility, and intellectual vocation in modern Europe.

Ranucci, Cristina

active 20th-21st century

Cristina Ranucci is an Italian art historian specializing in medieval painting, particularly the thirteenth and fourteenth centuries, with research interests that also extend to late antique archaeology. Her work is especially relevant for studies of Italian Duecento and Trecento visual culture and regional workshop traditions.

Ranucci is active as an independent art historian and lecturer, with a research profile centered on thirteenth- and fourteenth-century Italian painting. Her Academia.edu profile lists 17 published articles and identifies her principal interests as 13th- and 14th-century painting and late antique archaeology, situating her among independent scholars engaged in sustained scholarly production outside a permanent university chair.

Among her most significant contributions is the entry on Gentile da Rocca in Treccani's Dizionario Biografico degli Italiani (vol. 53, 2000), where she examines a painter active in Abruzzo in the second half of the thirteenth century. The study includes a detailed discussion of a 1283 Virgin Enthroned Nursing the Child, combining technical and iconographic analysis with attention to stylistic attributes, Gothic inscriptions, and regional context.

Her research also includes studies on thirteenth-century painting in Terni and the contribution La maesta di Otricoli, presented in the proceedings of the Third Week of Studies in Medieval Art History at the University of Rome. In collaboration with Monica Zorzan, she has worked on fragments of painted plaster from the presbytery of the ancient Basilica of St. Paul Outside the Walls, a topic of particular importance for the study of early medieval painted decoration. She also published The Hermitage of San Cataldo in Cottanello: From Research to Conservation (Miligraf, 2011), a volume that links historical-artistic investigation with conservation issues.

Recanati, Maria Grazia

Bergamo, 1962

Maria Grazia Recanati was born in Bergamo in 1962. She studied at the Università Cattolica del Sacro Cuore in Milan, where she graduated in lettere moderne with a specialization in art history and medieval history, writing her thesis on late medieval Lombard art history under the supervision of the distinguished Hungarian-Italian art historian Miklos Boskovits. After her degree she further refined her methodological training at the Fondazione "Roberto Longhi" in Florence, one of Italy's most prestigious institutes for the advanced study of art history.

Following her postgraduate formation at the Longhi Foundation, Recanati collaborated with the chair of medieval and modern art history at the Università Cattolica and simultaneously worked with the Pinacoteca Carrara in Bergamo. These early institutional ties established her dual profile as both a researcher engaged with the scholarly community and a museum professional committed to the public communication of art history. Her research output in this period included, notably, a 1992 study of the late-14th-century secular allegorical fresco cycle at the Castello di Bianzano in the Val Cavallina, a rare example of profane pictorial decoration in the Bergamo area that she was among the first scholars to analyze systematically.

Recanati's contribution to the 1998 volume Il Protiro settentrionale di Santa Maria Maggiore in Bergamo, edited by Enzo Mangili and published by the Banca Popolare di Bergamo, positioned her as a key voice in the scholarly reassessment of Giovanni da Campione's masterwork. The northern protiro, built between 1351 and 1353, is one of the finest examples of Campionese Gothic sculpture in Lombardy, featuring red Veronese marble lion-bearers and a rich sculptural program that connects Lombard Romanesque typology with the refined Trecento workshop tradition of the Campionesi. The volume, produced in conjunction with a major restoration campaign of the monument carried out between 1997 and 1998, brought together several specialists; among the contributors also noted in related scholarship is Saverio Lomartire, who addressed the theme of Giovanni di Ugo da Campione and the civic myth of the Campionesi.

The central axis of Recanati's scholarly activity has consistently been medieval Lombard painting and miniature. In 1989 she co-authored Codici e incunaboli miniati della Biblioteca civica di Bergamo, a foundational catalogue of the illuminated manuscripts held by Bergamo's civic library. She also contributed to the monumental multi-volume series La pittura in Lombardia, the Cariplo-sponsored survey coordinated by Mina Gregori and published between 1987 and 1999, where she co-authored with Carla Travi the section on Lombardy, Piedmont, and Liguria in the volume dedicated to mural painting from the late 13th to the early 15th century. In 2018 she delivered a public lecture at the Biblioteca Angelo Mai in Bergamo on the little-known illuminated Messale MA 298, using it as a lens to reconstruct the largely uncharted context of Lombard manuscript painting in the second half of the 13th century.

From 2000 to 2010, Recanati served as Director of the Accademia Carrara di Belle Arti di Bergamo, one of the major art academies in northern Italy. This decade-long directorship reinforced her standing as a central figure in Bergamo's cultural institutions. She has also served as a member of the Commission for Mobile Cultural Heritage of the Diocese of Bergamo, and as president of the cultural commission of the Biblioteca Angelo Mai, Bergamo's principal civic research library.

One of Recanati's most important recent scholarly contributions is the first volume of the scientific catalogue of the Accademia Carrara, dedicated to the panel paintings of the 14th and 15th centuries, published in September 2018. The catalogue was the result of years of archival and connoisseurial research and established significant new attributions within the collection, making it an essential reference tool for the study of Lombard and northern Italian painting of the Trecento and Quattrocento. She continues to teach art history at the Accademia di Belle Arti "G. Carrara" - now known as the Politecnico delle Arti di Bergamo - where her courses address medieval and modern art.

Richter, George Martin

San Francisco, 27 March 1875 - June 1942

George Martin Richter was born on 27 March 1875 in San Francisco, California, the son of Dr. Clemens Max Richter (1848-1936), a noted surgeon, and his wife Emma. He pursued his art-historical training in Germany, writing his doctoral dissertation on the sixteenth-century German painter Melchior Feselen under a professor described as a leading chair in art history and noted scholar of Bavarian art history from the Middle Ages to the early modern era. His doctorate was conferred in 1907, and it was during these formative German years that Richter absorbed the rigorous, object-centred methodology of the German academic tradition.

Richter's intellectual formation was decisively shaped by his encounter with Giovanni Morelli, the Italian senator and connoisseur who had revolutionized art historical attribution by insisting on the close examination of small, unconsciously rendered details - ears, hands, fingernails - as fingerprints of individual artistic personality. Richter famously introduced Morelli to Bernard Berenson, a meeting that proved consequential for the entire field of Italian Renaissance connoisseurship, as Berenson adapted and extended the Morellian method throughout his long career. This placed Richter at the very nerve centre of the connoisseurial world at the turn of the twentieth century.

From approximately 1910 to 1912, Richter worked at the Galerie Helbing, a prominent gallery and auction house, where his scholarly expertise and his growing photo archive were both developed and refined. In 1918 he returned to Munich and established his own art dealership, and in July 1919 he co-founded the luxury publishing company Phantasus-Verlag with H. H. Schlieper. Over the following years his scholarly attention shifted increasingly toward Italian Renaissance art, with a particular concentration on the Venetian master Giorgione and on Florentine painters of the fifteenth and early sixteenth centuries. On 24 March 1925, the Richters were invited to tea with Bernard and Mary Berenson at the Villa I Tatti in Settignano, cementing a connection between the two most prominent connoisseur-scholars of Italian art of the day.

While most art historians of his era collected photographs as part of their research practice, Richter accumulated them on an exceptional scale, ultimately assembling an archive of some sixty thousand photographs along with an unspecified quantity of press clippings, all arranged in hundreds of green slip-case boxes according to his own classification system: first by school, then by artist attribution, and finally by chronology. He recorded the system in a small leather-bound notebook that survives in the National Gallery of Art. This archive was conceived as a tool for connoisseurship and authentication - a visual memory bank against which new attributions could be tested - and it anticipated the kind of photographic reference libraries that would later become standard in major art research institutions.

With the rise of fascism in Italy, Richter relocated his family and his archive from Florence to England in 1929, continuing to write, lecture, and advise clients on Italian art from his new base. Eventually he emigrated to the United States, where Hilla von Rebay - the pioneering director of what would become the Guggenheim Museum - helped introduce him to the New York art world and encouraged him to resume his research. He took up again his work on the Florentine painter Andrea dal Castagno (a book eventually published posthumously) and began a new project linking Giorgione to the modernist painting movements of the early twentieth century.

Finding it difficult to establish himself as a dealer in New York and discovering that income from writing and lecturing was insufficient, Richter recognized that his library and archive were his most valuable assets. From February 1940 he began negotiating with David Finley, director of the National Gallery of Art in Washington, offering the entire archive of sixty thousand photographs for fifty thousand dollars. In February 1942, already in failing health, Richter presented his last paper at Johns Hopkins University, accompanying an exhibition on Giorgione. He died in June 1942. It was only after his death that Solomon Guggenheim, at the urging of Hilla von Rebay, stepped in and provided the funds for the National Gallery to purchase the archive from Mrs. Richter, while his books were sold at auction. In 1943 the National Gallery announced with great fanfare the donation of what it called the George M. Richter Archive of Illustrations on Art, emphasizing its vital role in authenticating works of art and establishing provenance in the turbulent aftermath of the Second World War.

Within this broader career, Richter's article Megliore di Jacopo and the Magdalen Master (Burlington Magazine, Vol. 57, No. 332, November 1930) represents his engagement with the problems of Duecento Florentine painting that naturally arose from his long residence in Florence during the 1920s. The article is entirely consistent with his method: placing anonymous and semi-anonymous painters within networks of stylistic affiliation, drawing on his vast photographic archive for comparison, and contributing to the same connoisseurial project that Offner and Berenson were simultaneously pursuing. For a scholar of your specialization, Alberto, the Richter Archive at the National Gallery of Art in Washington would itself be a remarkable resource - its sixty thousand photographs of Italian paintings, organized by school and artist, constitute an extraordinary document of how early-twentieth-century scholarship constructed the corpus of Italian medieval and Renaissance art.

Romagnoli, Ettore

Siena, 22 October 1772 - Siena, 13 January 1838

Ettore Romagnoli was one of the most versatile figures in early nineteenth-century Sienese culture: art historian, musicologist, composer, and interpreter of the city's heritage for Italian and foreign visitors. For scholars of medieval Sienese art, he remains an indispensable historical source, especially for the reconstruction of local artistic biographies and archival traditions.

Born in Siena, he remained closely tied to the city throughout his life and also performed a quasi-diplomatic cultural role, accompanying foreign dignitaries on behalf of local noble circles. He was a member of the Societa Colombaria Fiorentina, one of the major learned societies of Grand Ducal Italy, and collaborated with the music theorist Carlo Gervasoni on the drafting of the Nuova teoria di musica.

His magnum opus is the autograph manuscript Biografia cronologica de' Bellartisti senesi dal secolo XII a tutto il XVIII, organized in twelve volumes plus a thirteenth volume of indexes, for a total of roughly 11,000 pages. Donated by the author to the Municipal Library of Siena in 1835, it gathers biographies of painters, sculptors, architects, and artisans, with documentary transcriptions, archival references, and critical observations. The first volume, dedicated to artists active between 1100 and 1300, is directly relevant to research on Duecento and Trecento Siena.

The manuscript was used by contemporaries such as Rumohr, Gaye, and Milanesi, later noted by Julius von Schlosser in Letteratura artistica (1924), and consulted by John Pope-Hennessy in his 1937 study of Giovanni di Paolo. After long periods of relative neglect, it has returned to the center of scholarship thanks to the complete digital transcription project promoted by Fondazione Memofonte. A facsimile reprint in thirteen volumes was published by S.P.E.S. (Studio per Edizioni Scelte) in 1976.

Among his printed works, Romagnoli published the Guide to the City of Siena for Lovers of the Fine Arts (Siena, Marco Ferri e Figlio, 1832), one of the earliest systematic guides to the city's artistic heritage. Posthumously appeared the Historical and Artistic Notes on Siena and Its Suburbs (Siena, Onorato Porri, 1840), republished in a third edition in 1852 with a biographical essay on the author and his writings. Together with the manuscript corpus, these works confirm his role as a foundational mediator between local erudition and modern art-historical method.

Romano, Serena

Italian-Swiss scholar, active 20th–21st century

Serena Romano is an Italian-Swiss scholar and art historian best known for her work on medieval art, Roman visual culture, and Lombard art under the Visconti and Sforza. She combines institutional experience in the Italian Ministry of Culture with a long academic career in Switzerland, where she held a professorship in Medieval Art and is now emerita.

Romano studied art history at the University of Rome "La Sapienza," earning a degree focused on medieval art. She began her professional life as an art historian in the Italian Ministry of Culture, working in Venice and Rome, and later shifted into academia, becoming professor of medieval art at the University of Lausanne (Universite de Lausanne), where she served until her retirement and is now professor emerita. She has also taught in the "Art History in Rome" program at the University of Rome Tor Vergata, offering graduate-level courses on medieval visual culture.

Her research centers on painting and visual culture in Rome from Late Antiquity through the Middle Ages, as reflected in major synthetic books such as Arte e iconografia a Roma. Da Costantino a Cola di Rienzo and related volumes on Roman art from Late Antiquity to the end of the Middle Ages. She has written extensively on the Basilica of Santa Maria Maggiore and on the frescoes of the Lower Church at Assisi, where she was involved in early research projects on the Assisi fresco cycles. Another strong line of work concerns Lombard art under the Visconti and Sforza, including studies on arte lombarda and the role of court patronage in the later Middle Ages and early Renaissance.

Romano has published monographs and edited volumes with major Italian publishers such as Jaca Book and Vita e Pensiero, and her titles appear in both scholarly and museum-oriented catalogues. She has also curated or co-curated exhibitions, for example the 2015 show Arte lombarda dai Visconti agli Sforza, which brought together paintings, manuscripts, and decorative arts to trace the development of Lombard visual culture under those dynasties. Her bibliography ranges from technical studies of individual fresco cycles to broader synthetic accounts of Roman and Italian medieval art, making her a key reference for students and researchers working on Italian medieval painting and iconography.

Rotili, Mario

Benevento, 12 August 1920 - Benevento, 26 February 1981

Mario Rotili was one of the most multifaceted figures in twentieth-century Southern Italian culture: a historian of medieval and modern art, museologist, librarian, archivist, and politician, he combined a distinguished academic career with direct civic commitment to the reconstruction of his city. His figure uniquely combines scientific research with administrative responsibility, in an intellectual profile reminiscent of certain civic humanists of the fifteenth century.

He was born in Benevento on August 12, 1920, and spent most of his life there. After attending a classical high school in his hometown, he enrolled in the Faculty of Letters at the University of Naples, where he earned his degree and took his first steps in art-historical research. He also began teaching art history at the classical high school in Benevento and contributed to local newspapers with articles on literary, theatrical, and film criticism, revealing from a young age a cultural openness that would characterize his entire body of work.

A member of the Christian Democrats, Rotili entered city politics at a delicate moment: between 1952 and 1956 he led the opposition to right-wing administrations, and in 1956 he was elected mayor of Benevento, a position he held for seven years, until June 1963. His term was marked by a measured and careful policy of reconstruction of the areas destroyed by Anglo-American bombing in 1943, with a General Urban Plan entrusted to the renowned urban planner Luigi Piccinato. He took stock of that period in the book Seven Years in the Service of My City (Benevento, Abete, 1963), and for his civic commitment he was awarded the title of Grand Officer of the Order of Merit of the Italian Republic. Nearly ninety percent of the City Council's resolutions were approved unanimously during his tenure, a sign of his exceptional ability to mediate.

In 1959, while still serving as mayor, Rotili assumed the directorship of the city's cultural institutions: the Museo del Sannio, the Provincial Library, and the Provincial Historical Archives of Benevento. During his eleven years as director, until 1970, he completely reorganized the museum, established an art gallery within it, divided it into four specialized sections, and promoted exhibitions, conferences, concerts, and meetings with leading figures in Italian and international culture, envisioning the museum as a public hub of cultural life and not merely a repository of objects. He oversaw the restoration of the rich collection of parchment materials preserved in the museum's holdings, and for this work he was awarded the Gold Medal of Merit for Education, Culture, and the Arts by presidential decree in 1965.

After obtaining the title of Lecturer in Medieval and Modern Art History, Rotili initially taught at the Academy of Fine Arts in Naples, then at the University of Naples as an adjunct professor, and finally as a full professor, holding the chair of History of Miniature and Minor Arts and subsequently of Medieval and Modern Art History. He also taught at the Suor Orsola Benincasa University Institute of Education in Naples and was a lecturer in the Advanced Culture Courses at the Italian University for Foreigners in Perugia. A full member of the Pontaniana Academy and the National Society of Sciences, Letters, and Arts of Naples, he was also an Honorary Academician of the Pontifical Insigne Academy of the Virtuosi al Pantheon.

The core of Rotili's work is the history of medieval art in southern Italy, with Benevento and the Lombard duchy at the center of his focus. His first major volume, L'Arte nel Sannio (Benevento, 1952), won the Premio Napoli for Art History in 1956. This was followed by a series of studies on early medieval architecture and sculpture in the Benevento area, culminating in the monumental edition of L'Arco di Traiano a Benevento (Rome, Istituto Poligrafico dello Stato, 1972), which examined one of the most important monuments of classical art in Southern Italy. The volume Byzantine Art in Calabria and Basilicata (Cava dei Tirreni, Di Mauro, 1980) broadened the scope to encompass the landscape of late antique and medieval art in southern Italy. Rotili also contributed to the Corpus della scultura altomedioevale edited by the Italian Center for Studies on the Early Middle Ages in Spoleto, for which he authored the volume dedicated to the Diocese of Benevento (1966).

A second, equally original line of research concerns the history of miniature painting: Rotili was among the first Italian scholars to devote systematic research to book illustration in medieval Southern Italy. His major works in this field include La miniatura gotica in Italia (Naples, Libreria Scientifica, 1968-1969), Introduzione alla storia della miniatura e delle arti minori in Italia (1970, with Antonella Putaturo Murano), The Illuminated Dante Manuscripts of Naples (1972) and, above all, the two-volume work Miniature Painting in the Abbey of Cava (Cava dei Tirreni, Di Mauro, 1976-1978), dedicated to the Benedictine scriptorium of the Abbey of the Trinity in Cava. In 1963, he published Origins of Italian Painting (Bergamo, Italian Institute of Graphic Arts), a richly illustrated volume offering an overview of Italian painting from its Christian origins through the High Middle Ages, in which he included, among others, G. Salvi's thesis on the true identity of the painter Conxolus of the Sacro Speco in Subiaco.

His interest in architecture is also evident in his studies on Filippo Raguzzini, the Neapolitan architect of the Roman Rococo, to whom Rotili dedicated a volume in 1951 and a posthumous concluding essay in 1982; and in the series of studies on Luigi Vanvitelli, beginning in 1954, which culminated in the critical edition of the Vita di Luigi Vanvitelli written by his son (Naples, Banca di Roma-S.E.N., 1975). He contributed numerous entries to the Dizionario Biografico degli Italiani and the Enciclopedia Dantesca, and contributed to the major collective work L'art dans l'Italie meridionale, the updated edition of Emile Bertaux's monumental work edited by Adriano Prandi (Rome, École française, 1978).

Mario Rotili died in his hometown of Benevento on February 26, 1981, at the age of sixty. One year after his passing, the City Administration, the Provincial Administration, and the Rotary Club organized an official commemoration, the proceedings of which were published in 1987. In 1984, friends and students published Studi di Storia dell'Arte in memoria di Mario Rotili (Naples, Banca Sannitica, 2 vols., 954 pp.), a miscellaneous collection featuring his complete bibliography, edited by Alessandra Perriccioli Saggese, and a tribute by the great historian Giuseppe Galasso. The City of Benevento later named a street in his honor.

S

Salmi, Mario

San Giovanni Valdarno, 14 June 1889 – Rome, 16 November 1980

Mario Salmi was one of the most influential Italian art historians of the twentieth century, specializing in Romanesque architecture, Tuscan sculpture, and the early Italian Renaissance. Born in San Giovanni Valdarno on 14 June 1889, he died in Rome on 16 November 1980, leaving behind a scholarly legacy of extraordinary breadth and depth.

In 1910, Salmi earned his law degree from the University of Pisa with a pioneering thesis titled La tutela del patrimonio artistico nazionale, demonstrating from the very beginning an exceptional sensitivity toward the protection of cultural heritage. From 1911 to 1914, he pursued advanced studies in Art History at the University of Rome under the guidance of Adolfo Venturi, one of the founding fathers of the discipline in Italy. This rigorous training laid the foundation for a philological method that would characterize his entire career: always starting from a direct and meticulous observation of the work — "stone by stone," as he liked to say — and then arriving at critical assessments within a broader historical perspective.

In 1917 he taught Art History at the Academy of Fine Arts in Parma, and beginning in 1918 he served as inspector of monuments in various Italian regions, from Puglia to Molise, from Umbria to Lombardy. In 1923 he became a professor at the University of Pisa, where he founded the Institute of Art History, which opened in 1929. He then moved to the University of Florence, where he taught from 1929 to 1949 and where he established the National Institute of Renaissance Studies in 1937. From 1950 to 1964, he held the chair of Medieval Art History at the University of Rome, where he was named professor emeritus upon his retirement.

His institutional influence extended far beyond the university lecture halls. In 1952, he was among the founding members of the Italian Center for Studies on the Early Middle Ages, an institution that helped bring a historical period previously considered marginal back into the center of scholarly attention. In 1949 he founded the art history and criticism journal Commentari, and was also co-editor of the journal Rinascimento. He served as editor-in-chief of the monumental fifteen-volume Enciclopedia Universale dell'Arte, published between 1958 and 1967, promptly translated into English as Encyclopedia of World Art by McGraw-Hill in New York.

Salmi's most distinctive trait was his tireless visual empiricism: at a time when the aesthetic perspective marked by Croce's idealism dominated in Italy, he instead favored a rigorous historical-philological approach, always accompanied by a direct examination of the works. To reach monuments scattered across the most remote countryside, when few roads were yet passable, Salmi would walk dozens of kilometers, sometimes spending the night in farmhouses. During visits to restoration sites, those working there recalled the arrival of this tall, solemn figure who "immediately and rapidly observed, read, and analyzed everything, comparing it with distant monuments, aided by a prodigious memory." His writings, even the most specialized ones, are characterized by a linear and essential prose, accessible even to non-specialists — which reflected his conviction that formal clarity was an expression of an orderly and harmonious vision of reality.

The chronological scope of Salmi's scholarly work ranges from early Christian and Coptic art to the Baroque, spanning architecture, sculpture, painting, and the so-called minor arts. His primary fields of expertise, however, were the Romanesque and the Renaissance: the two-volume set Romanesque Architecture in Tuscany and Romanesque Sculpture in Tuscany (1926–1927) remains a fundamental reference in the field to this day. Salmi was one of the first scholars to devote academic attention to the minor arts, a field then still neglected in academic circles, even establishing a chair for it at the University of Florence.

A field to which Salmi devoted particular attention was that of Italian medieval miniature painting. Notable among his works on the subject are La miniatura fiorentina gotica (1954), Italian Miniatures (1954, translated into English and published by H. N. Abrams), La miniatura italiana (1956, Electa), and the study of the Grimani Breviary (1974). These works earned him international recognition as the foremost authority on the tradition of Italian miniature painting.

His deepest passion remained Piero della Francesca. In 1979, at the age of ninety, he published his last and most laborious work, The Painting of Piero della Francesca, confessing that he had "exhausted what remained of his extraordinary vitality" in it. His student Umberto Baldini recalled that his love for Piero had been "one of the focal points of his life as a scholar and teacher," almost a symbol of the order and divine harmony of the Renaissance.

His career was not without controversy. When he was chosen over Roberto Longhi for the chair of Art History at the University of Rome, Bernard Berenson uttered the famous — and ruthless — phrase: "A genius has been neglected in favor of an insect." Salmi was also involved in the controversial affair surrounding the breakup of the Contini-Bonacossi Collection, which led to the export of numerous masterpieces (including works by Zurbarán, Piero della Francesca, and Giovanni Bellini), resulting in parliamentary inquiries and a complaint for "fraud against the state." No less combative was his stance regarding the doors of Orvieto Cathedral, for which he resigned as vice president of the Higher Council of Antiquities and Fine Arts when there were plans to include a work by Emilio Greco.

Salmi was a member of the Accademia Nazionale dei Lincei — to which he bequeathed his vast library, including numerous manuscripts, upon his death — and a foreign member of the Académie de France. He served as president of the Accademia Petrarca di Lettere, Arti e Scienze in Arezzo from 1945 to 1979, transforming it into an institution of international significance. He received the Gold Medal of Merit for Education, Culture, and the Arts, and an honorary doctorate from the University of Algiers. He donated a significant portion of his private art collection to the Museum of Medieval and Modern Art in Arezzo.

Among his most illustrious students were Enzo Carli, Paola Barocchi, Umberto Baldini, Vittoria Kienerk, and Giovanni Carandente, a school that profoundly shaped Italian art history in the second half of the twentieth century. At the age of eighty-five, he still regretted no longer being able to work fourteen to fifteen hours a day as he had done for decades — eloquent testimony to a total dedication to art history that has few parallels in the Italian academic landscape.

Salvini, Roberto

Modena, 1912 – Florence, 1985

Roberto Salvini was one of the most important Italian art historians of the twentieth century, making fundamental contributions to the study of medieval art, Romanesque sculpture, and Flemish painting. A student of the medievalist Mario Salmi, Salvini received his education in Florence, Munich, and Berlin, acquiring a European methodological perspective that would characterize his entire body of work. In 1937, he joined the Fine Arts administration, serving as superintendent of galleries in various locations — Trento, Palermo, and Modena — during the years of World War II. His institutional career culminated in his directorship of the Uffizi Gallery in Florence, where he promoted significant museographic innovations.

In 1956, Salvini began his university career as a professor at the University of Trieste, before moving to the University of Florence, where he taught for many years. He was actively engaged in the methodology of teaching art history, a topic he had already addressed in his theoretical essay La pura visibilità (1947), which reflected the influence of the German formalist tradition on his education. In 1983, he was elected a National Member of the Accademia dei Lincei, the highest academic honour in Italy. He also served as President of the Art History Section of the Accademia delle Arti del Disegno in Florence and held the position of Secretary General.

His studies ranged from Italian medieval art to the Renaissance, from Flemish art to European Romanesque sculpture. Among his most significant works in medieval studies are Medieval Mosaics in Sicily (1949), Wiligelmo and the Origins of Romanesque Sculpture in Europe (1956) — a pioneering study on the origins of Romanesque sculpture — and The Cloister of Monreale (1962). He also made significant contributions to Byzantine studies, writing seminal volumes such as The Cathedral of Modena (1966) and the volume Medieval Sculpture for the international series History of Western Sculpture.

A recurring theme throughout his work was his interest in the relationship between Italian patrons and Flemish painting, explored both in Flemish Painting (1958) and in his last published work, Florentine Bankers and Flemish Painters (1985), released in the year of his death. Salvini left a profound mark on half a century of national and international art history, and in 2018 and 2022 his legacy was celebrated with conferences and publications dedicated to his scholarly contributions.

Sandberg-Vavalà, Evelyn

Compton, Berkshire, 1888 – Florence, 8 September 1961

Evelyn Sandberg-Vavalà was born Evelyn May Graham Sandberg in Compton, Berkshire, in 1888. She was the only child of the Reverend George Alfred Sandberg (1848-1910), vicar of the church of Saints Mary and Nicholas, and Annie Sandberg (1858-1894), who died when Evelyn was still a child. She studied geography and went on to teach in a girls' grammar school and at a university college — an education and career path typical of the small number of professionally active women of her generation in Britain. She was also known by her married name, Evelyn Kendrew, though it was her pseudonym, Sandberg-Vavalà, that she used exclusively in her scholarly life.

In 1921 Sandberg-Vavalà made the decisive choice of her intellectual life: she moved to Florence, where she would remain for thirty-five years — interrupted only by the years of the Second World War. In Florence she placed herself under the guidance of Bernard Berenson, the great Lithuanian-American connoisseur and art historian who had established his villa I Tatti as the pre-eminent centre for the study of Italian Renaissance painting. Under Berenson's influence she developed into a rigorous scholar of iconography and early Italian painting, and she established close intellectual relationships with the most important art historians of the age: John Pope-Hennessy, Roberto Longhi, and Giuseppe Fiocco. She became well known in Florence not only as a researcher but also as a tutor and guide for students of art at the Galleria degli Uffizi, accompanying them on visits throughout Italy.

Her first and most enduring major work was published in 1929: La Croce Dipinta Italiana e l'Iconografia della Passione (Italian Painted Crucifixes and the Iconography of the Passion). This book remains a foundational reference in the study of the medieval painted cross — the croce dipinta — a distinctly Italian devotional form that was central to the visual culture of the Duecento and Trecento. For the first time, Sandberg-Vavalà subjected the entire corpus of surviving Italian painted crucifixes to systematic iconographic and stylistic analysis, tracing the development of the form from the Christus Triumphans of the Romanesque period to the suffering Christus Patiens of the Gothic age. Her work in this field was so foundational that a full century later, the Römisches Jahrbuch of the Bibliotheca Hertziana published a special section entitled Aspekte der Tafelkreuzforschung im Jahrhundert nach Evelyn Sandberg-Vavalà — "Aspects of panel cross research in the century after Evelyn Sandberg-Vavalà", a tribute to the lasting relevance of her pioneering study.

In 1934 she published her study on the iconography of the Virgin and Child in thirteenth-century Italian painting: L'iconografia della Madonna col bambino nella pittura italiana del Dugento, a work that brought the same systematic iconographic method she had applied to the crucifix tradition to bear on the Madonna type. She also made important contributions to the study of Venetian Trecento painting: she is cited by scholars of Venetian art — alongside Roberto Longhi — for her analyses of the workshop of Paolo Veneziano and his followers. Her 1926 monograph La pittura veronese del Trecento established her as one of the first scholars to treat Veronese medieval painting with the rigour it deserved.

In the postwar years Sandberg-Vavalà produced two major synthetic works that confirmed her reputation as the leading English-language authority on central Italian medieval and early Renaissance painting. Uffizi Studies: The Development of the Florentine School of Painting appeared in 1948, followed by Sienese Studies: The Development of the School of Painting of Siena in 1953. Both works combined iconographic analysis with careful attention to stylistic development and workshop tradition, and both remain useful reference tools. Her final monograph, Studies in Florentine Churches (Olschki, Florence, 1959), extended the investigation to the specific devotional and institutional contexts in which Florentine painting was produced and displayed.

One of the most remarkable legacies of Sandberg-Vavalà's scholarly life was her photographic archive of over 25,000 photographs of Italian paintings from the thirteenth to the sixteenth centuries. This archive, which she assembled and organised over four decades, constitutes an extraordinary documentary resource covering medieval mosaics and frescoes, Romanesque sculpture, and applied arts including cassoni, ivories, and jewellery. From 1957, aware of her declining health, Federico Zeri worked to ensure that the archive would be preserved intact. He persuaded Count Vittorio Cini to purchase it, and the archive passed after her death to the Fondazione Giorgio Cini in Venice, where it is preserved today as the Fondo Sandberg-Vavalà. A further portion of her photographic materials is held at the Fondazione Federico Zeri at the Università di Bologna.

Sandberg-Vavalà died in Florence on 8 September 1961, cared for in her final illness by the nuns of a religious community. She was buried in the small cemetery of Moggiona, a hamlet of Poppi in the Casentino, the forested valley southeast of Florence that she evidently loved. She was seventy-three years old. Her scholarly achievement was remarkable not only for its range and depth but for the era in which it was accomplished: as a self-taught British woman working in Florence in the interwar years, without a university chair or institutional support, she produced a body of work that shaped the study of medieval Italian painting for generations. The centenary of her great work on the painted crucifix has been marked by new international scholarship, the best possible tribute to an historian whose questions have not yet been fully answered.

Schubert, Ernst

Bemerode near Hannover, 23 May 1941 - Hannover, 18 March 2006

Ernst Schubert was a German historian best known for his work in medieval and regional history. He spent most of his academic life at the University of Gottingen, where from 1985 until his death he held the chair in Niedersachsische Landesgeschichte, the regional history of Lower Saxony.

Schubert studied history and the auxiliary historical disciplines, with a strong focus on the Middle Ages, and completed both his doctorate and habilitation in this field. After several academic appointments, he became Professor fur Niedersachsische Landesgeschichte at Gottingen in 1985, where he helped shape regional history as a major area of research and supervised numerous doctoral students.

His main research interests lay in the social and constitutional history of the Middle Ages and the early modern period, especially in Lower Saxony and northwestern Germany. He also worked on environmental and agrarian history, examining how land use, settlement patterns, and legal institutions influenced medieval society.

Schubert's studies regularly combined close archival research with a sustained focus on institutions such as lordships, towns, and ecclesiastical jurisdictions. This methodological combination made his work particularly valuable for understanding regional structures of power and long-term historical change.

He remained active in research and teaching until shortly before his death in 2006 at the age of sixty-four. His books and edited volumes continue to be cited in scholarship on medieval and early modern German regional history, especially for studies concerning Lower Saxony and neighboring territories.

Schmidt, Victor Michael

active late 20th-21st century; retired May 2024

Victor Michael Schmidt is a Dutch art historian specializing in Italian medieval and Renaissance painting, with a particular focus on panel painting, the typology and function of art objects, iconography, and the origins of art theory. For decades, he served as Associate Professor of Art History at Utrecht University, where he remained until his retirement in May 2024.

Schmidt's research focuses on Italian painting of the 13th and 14th centuries, employing an approach that integrates typological analysis, liturgical function, and the private devotion associated with pictorial objects. He is one of the foremost living specialists in late medieval Italian panel painting, with a specific focus on the Marche, Tuscany, and the Umbrian region.

His perspective is distinctly interdisciplinary: in addition to stylistic attribution, he investigates the artist's social status, patronage, and the reception of the works within the religious and civil context of the Middle Ages.

Schmidt's most influential publication is his editorship of the volume Italian Panel Painting of the Duecento and Trecento (Studies in the History of Art, 61), published by the National Gallery of Art in Washington in 2002, which brings together twenty-three essays by international specialists and grew out of a symposium held in Florence in 1998.

This volume has become an indispensable reference tool for anyone studying the origins of Italian panel painting. Equally significant is Painted Piety: Panel Paintings for Personal Devotion in Tuscany, 1250-1400 (Centro Di, Florence, 2005), a monographic study on private devotion and the relationship between patron and sacred image in 13th- and 14th-century Tuscany. In 2010, he co-edited with Justin Kroesen The Altar and Its Environment, 1150-1400 (Brepols, Turnhout), a collective volume on the altar as a physical, liturgical, and visual space in the Middle Ages. More recently, he published Processional Banners and Gonfalons from the Marche. Late Middle Ages and Renaissance (Edizioni Centro Studi "G. Mazzini," Fermignano, 2020), exploring processional painting in the Marche region.

Schmidt is a member of the editorial boards of some of the most authoritative Italian journals of medieval and Renaissance art history, including Prospettiva, La Diana (journal of the School of Specialization in Historical and Artistic Heritage at the University of Siena), Arte marchigiana, and Studi montefeltrani.

He is also a member of the board of directors of the Stichting voor Nederlandse Kunsthistorische Publicaties, the Dutch organization that publishes the journal Simiolus, one of the most respected art history publications in the Netherlands. He has maintained academic ties with the Dutch University Institute of Art History in Florence, an institution that has traditionally fostered the relationship between Dutch art historiography and Italian art.

Until his retirement, Schmidt trained entire generations of art historians in Utrecht, supervising theses and research on topics related to medieval Italian painting. As evidenced by his students still active in 2025-2026, his influence extended far beyond his direct teaching: a recent example is the presentation of a bachelor's thesis on Francesco da Bologna at Utrecht University in May 2026, supervised by Schmidt himself. The National Gallery of Art in Washington has also included his bibliography in online catalogs dedicated to 13th- and 14th-century Italian painting, a recognition of the institutional significance of his work.

Schapiro, Meyer

Šiauliai, 1904 – New York, 1996

Pioneering American art historian, critic, and teacher whose interdisciplinary approach revolutionised the study of medieval, Romanesque, and modern art. Born on September 23, 1904, in Šiauliai, Lithuania, he immigrated to the United States with his family in 1907, settling in New York City, where he was profoundly shaped by the city's vibrant intellectual and artistic milieu.

Schapiro attended Columbia University, earning his bachelor's degree in 1924, his master's in 1926, and his Ph.D. in 1929 with a groundbreaking dissertation on twelfth-century Romanesque sculpture in Burgundy, which challenged prevailing stylistic chronologies and emphasised socio-political contexts. His early work — including articles on Mozarabic art and cloister frescoes at Santo Domingo de Silos — established him as a meticulous scholar who integrated formal analysis with historical, theological, and economic factors, methods that contrasted sharply with the era's more rigid iconographic or formalist schools. Joining Columbia's faculty in 1928, he taught there for over fifty years, mentoring figures including Arthur Danto and Robert Rosenblum, and fostering dialogues between art history, Marxism, psychoanalysis, and phenomenology.

His seminal publications include Romanesque Art (1977) and essays collected in Words and Pictures (1973), which dissected the emotional expressivity in medieval sculpture — arguing, for instance, that the "chaotic" sculptural heads at Moissac reflected deliberate spiritual intensity rather than primitivism — as well as later monographs on Van Gogh (1950) and Cézanne (1952), which humanised modern masters by linking their innovations to personal psychology and cultural upheaval. His insistence on viewing art as a dynamic interplay of form, content, and human agency — epitomised in his critique of Erwin Panofsky's iconology — bridged medieval monastic traditions with twentieth-century abstraction, influencing fields from semiotics to New York School painting; he was personally acquainted with artists including Willem de Kooning. He died on March 26, 1996, leaving an enduring corpus that taught generations to see art not as isolated objects but as vital expressions of historical consciousness.

Scudieri, Magnolia

active late 20th-21st century

Magnolia Scudieri is an Italian art historian specialising in Renaissance and medieval art, particularly known for her scholarship on Beato Angelico and fourteenth- and fifteenth-century miniature painting. She devoted the greater part of her career to the Museo di San Marco in Florence, where she held key positions in conservation, restoration, and curation.

Scudieri earned her degree in Medieval and Modern History from the University of Florence, where she studied under Giorgio Bonsanti, whose assistant she served from 1982 to 1992. She began her professional career at the Ministry of Cultural Heritage, working in the Restoration Office of the Superintendency for Artistic and Historical Heritage of Florence and Pistoia, with specific assignments in the Fiesole area and at the Museo di San Marco. This initial phase allowed her to combine scientific rigour with curatorial practice, and her work on restorations revealed previously unknown aspects of Renaissance interiors, including the original green colour of the walls of Michelozzo's library, which emerged in 2000 from beneath a nineteenth-century patina.

From 1992 until her retirement after approximately thirty years of service, Scudieri directed the Museo di San Marco, transforming it into a centre of excellence for conservation and public engagement. Under her leadership, the museum oversaw restorations of the Sala Greca — including the polychrome wooden ceiling — and of key works by Beato Angelico: the San Marco Altarpiece, the Crucifixion in the Chapter House, and the Annalena Altarpiece. She reorganised sections of the collection, including the tombstones from San Pancrazio and the paintings by Fra' Bartolomeo, improving security, accessibility, and conservation standards while preserving the site's monastic character.

A specialist in fourteenth- and fifteenth-century manuscript illumination, she contributed to exhibition catalogues in Italy and abroad, curating exhibitions on subjects including the illuminated choir books of San Marco and the relationship between Beato Angelico and miniature painting. Her publications include Gli affreschi dell'Angelico a San Marco (2004), Fra Giovanni Angelico. Pittore miniatore o miniatore pittore? (2007), the biographical entry on Guido di Pietro (Beato Angelico) in the Dizionario Biografico degli Italiani (2004), and the official museum guide Museo di San Marco. La guida ufficiale (1999). Her research on Sister Plautilla Nelli's Lamentation over the Dead Christ contributed to the rediscovery of Nelli as the first Florentine female painter, a contribution recognised by the Nelli Prize awarded in 2004 by Advancing Women Artists.

Antonio Paolucci, former superintendent of the Florentine museums, praised Scudieri's ability to combine scientific rigour with humanity. Her "diachronic" approach to the museum — emphasising San Marco's spiritual and historical continuity from the Renaissance to the twentieth century — has been widely acknowledged as a defining contribution to the site's identity. She led distinguished tours, including that for Emperor Akihito of Japan in 1993.

Sinkević, Ida

active late 20th-21st century

Ida Sinkević is one of the world's leading specialists in late antique, Byzantine, and medieval art and architecture. A professor at Lafayette College in Pennsylvania, she is especially known for her monographic study of the Church of Saint Panteleimon at Nerezi, the monument founded by Alexios Angelos Komnenos in 1164.

Sinkević earned her Ph.D. in Art and Archaeology from Princeton University in 1994. Her dissertation was devoted entirely to the church at Nerezi and offered a systematic analysis of the monument's architecture, painting, and sculpture. Princeton, with its historic ties to classical and late antique studies and its proximity to the Index of Christian Art (now the Index of Medieval Art), provided an ideal environment for research that combined architectural history, pictorial iconography, and Byzantine aristocratic patronage. The choice of Nerezi as a dissertation topic was an ambitious one: although the monument had been discussed in the specialized literature, especially by Okunev in the 1920s and by André Grabar, it had never before received an exhaustive monographic treatment in English.

After completing her doctorate, Sinkević pursued her academic career at Lafayette College in Easton, Pennsylvania, where she holds the Arthur J. '55 and Barbara S. Rothkopf Professorship of Art History, a privately endowed chair recognizing the significance of her work. Within the liberal arts environment of Lafayette, she has developed a teaching program spanning an unusually broad chronological and geographical range: from Greek and Roman art to post-revolutionary Russian art, from early Christian architecture to medieval Serbian miniature painting.

From the outset, her research has been marked by an approach that integrates formal and stylistic analysis of monuments, the study of literary and liturgical sources, and close attention to patronage and strategies of self-representation. This method, indebted in part to Panofsky's iconological tradition while also drawing on the social history of art, is evident both in her monograph on Nerezi and in her subsequent articles.

Sinkević's seminal book is The Church of St. Panteleimon at Nerezi: Architecture, Programme, Patronage, published in 2000 by Ludwig Reichert in Wiesbaden. The 209-page volume, accompanied by 88 figures and 27 plates, systematically develops the themes of her doctoral dissertation: the analysis of the church's architecture in its typological context, the description and interpretation of the pictorial iconographic program, and, in a particularly original way, the reconstruction of the personality and motivations of the patron, Alexios Angelos Komnenos. In this book, and in the precursor article published in Gesta in 1996, Alexios Angelos Komnenos, a Patron without History?, Sinkević advanced the thesis that Alexios consciously adopted the surname Komnenos as a strategy of social legitimation, and that the church's iconographic program was aligned with theological positions independent of the imperial court of Manuel I.

Among her later contributions, the essay Western Chapels in Middle Byzantine Churches: Meaning and Significance, published in Starinar in 2002, stands out for its examination of the liturgical and compositional function of western chapels in Middle Byzantine churches, including the northwestern chapel at Nerezi. She has also published studies on Serbian royal mausoleums as sites of cultural identity construction for the Nemanjić dynasty, and on five-domed churches in Middle and Late Byzantine architecture.

In recent years, Sinkević has broadened her interests to include innovative technological applications in medieval art history. She collaborates with ZeuxisVR, a virtual reality project dedicated to the reconstruction and interpretation of historical monuments, where she serves as a senior researcher with expertise in Byzantine and medieval art. This work reflects a sustained commitment to widening access to medieval artistic heritage, especially monuments preserved in geographically remote locations such as Nerezi itself in North Macedonia.

Sinkević is also active as an editor of major collective volumes. Her most recent and significant editorial project is Type and Archetype in Late Antique and Byzantine Art and Architecture, published by Brill in 2023 and edited together with Jelena Bogdanović, Marina Mihaljević, and Čedomila Marinković. Bringing together papers first presented at the 23rd International Congress of Byzantine Studies in Belgrade in 2016, the volume addresses the problem of typology in late antique and Byzantine art, namely the relationship between model and archetype, across case studies ranging from early Christian architecture to late Palaiologan painting. The project confirms Sinkević's place among the leading contemporary scholars of Byzantine art and architecture working across the Anglo-American and Central European scholarly worlds.

Singer, Charles Joseph

London, 2 November 1876 - 10 June 1960

Charles Joseph Singer was a British physician, historian of science, and one of the founding figures of the academic discipline of the history of medicine. Over the course of a long career that bridged active medical practice and painstaking archival scholarship, he became the most internationally prominent English-speaking historian of science and technology of the first half of the twentieth century - a man whose work touched directly on medieval illuminated manuscripts, early anatomical texts, and the transmission of ancient knowledge.

Singer was born on 2 November 1876 in London, the son of the Reverend Simeon Singer, a Hebrew scholar of considerable distinction, and Charlotte Pyke. He was educated at the City of London School and then entered University College London in 1896 before winning a scholarship to Magdalen College, Oxford, where he read zoology and took his BA in 1900. Medicine was always his true aim, however, and he completed his clinical training at St. Mary's Hospital, qualifying in 1903. Almost immediately upon qualifying, he departed as medical officer on an expedition under Sir John Harrington to delimit the Abyssinian frontier with Sudan, spending nearly a year collecting biological specimens and studying medical cases in the field.

Singer's transformation from practicing physician to professional historian of medicine was set in motion in 1910, when browsing a second-hand bookshop he encountered a copy of Benjamin Marten's early eighteenth-century text on contagion and was captivated by the problem of how scientific ideas are transmitted across centuries. He began writing papers on medical history alongside his hospital work at the Dreadnought Hospital for Seamen and the Cancer Hospital, Brompton. In the spring of 1914, at the age of thirty-eight, he made the decisive and financially costly choice to give up consulting medicine entirely, accepting an invitation from Sir William Osler, then Regius Professor of Medicine at Oxford, to devote himself full-time to historical research. Singer and his wife Dorothy, largely at their own expense, established a history of science research room in the Radcliffe Camera at Oxford, installing a safe to hold manuscripts and opening it as a working space for researchers.

After serving in the Royal Army Medical Corps during the First World War, Singer returned to Oxford as Lecturer in the History of the Biological Sciences. When Osler died in 1919, the hopes of securing Singer an Oxford professorial chair died with him, and in 1920 he moved to University College London, where he was appointed Lecturer on the History of Medicine, a post which the University of London upgraded to an honorary professorship in 1930. He retired as Professor Emeritus in 1942. His international standing was extraordinary: he presided over the Third International Congress of the History of Medicine in 1922 and the Second International Congress of the History of Science and Technology in 1931. He lectured at Johns Hopkins and the University of Pennsylvania, and was visiting professor at the University of California in 1931-1932. He held Oxford doctorates in all three faculties - medicine, letters, and science - a distinction without parallel among British scholars of his generation.

Singer's bibliography spans an extraordinary range. His early collaboration with Dorothy on The Development of the Doctrine of Contagium Vivum, 1500-1750 (1913) established his method: close reading of primary sources, careful attention to manuscript transmission, and resistance to teleological narratives of progress. His two-volume edited collection Studies in the History and Method of Science (1917-1921) gathered the most important international scholarship of the era. A Short History of Anatomy from the Greeks to Harvey (1925, revised 1957) remains a foundational text. His edition and translation of A Prelude to Modern Science (1946), presenting the complete text of Vesalius's Tabulae anatomicae sex of 1538 with detailed analysis, was one of his crowning achievements.

Perhaps his most enduring monument is the monumental A History of Technology (five volumes, 1954-1958), which Singer co-edited and to which he contributed substantially, covering the full sweep of human technological history from prehistoric times through the Industrial Revolution. His From Magic to Science (1928), especially relevant to students of medieval intellectual history, examined the scientific content of illuminated manuscripts from the medieval period, particularly medical and natural-philosophic manuscripts, bringing textual scholarship into productive dialogue with art-historical analysis.

No account of Charles Singer's work can omit his wife, Dorothy Waley Singer (1882-1964), a scholar of Jewish history, Giordano Bruno, and manuscript cataloguing who was an equal intellectual partner throughout their shared life. She participated in virtually every major research project, sustained the correspondence networks that kept the Radcliffe Camera research room functioning during the war years, and produced her own substantial independent scholarship on Renaissance science and Hermeticism.

During the 1930s and 1940s, Singer's commitments extended well beyond the archive. He helped found the Society for the Protection of Science and Learning, the principal British organisation dedicated to rescuing Jewish and anti-fascist scholars fleeing Nazi persecution, and he and Dorothy personally sheltered and assisted many academic refugees. In 1956 he received the Osler Medal of Oxford University and shortly afterward the George Sarton Medal of the American Society for the History of Science - the two highest honours in his field. A Festschrift of ninety essays in his honour, Science, Medicine and History (1953), gathered contributions from leading historians of science on both sides of the Atlantic. Singer died on 10 June 1960, leaving behind a discipline he had done more than almost anyone else to establish as a rigorous and internationally recognized academic field.

Sirén, Osvald

Helsinki, April 6, 1879 – Stockholm, June 26, 1966

Osvald Sirén was one of the most important and versatile art historians of the twentieth century, a figure bridging the European tradition of connoisseurship and the new horizons of scientific Orientalism, and the author of a monumental body of work that uniquely encompasses both medieval and Renaissance Italian painting and Chinese art.

Born in Helsinki on April 6, 1879, into Finland's Swedish-speaking minority, Sirén grew up in a culturally vibrant environment open to the most advanced European intellectual currents. He completed his university studies in art history, training in the tradition of the connoisseurship method of Morelli and Berenson, which in those years dominated the discipline throughout Europe. From the outset, his scholarly work was deeply influenced by the formalist methods of Bernard Berenson, with whom he shared an interest in medieval Tuscan painters and Leonardo da Vinci, aiming to situate works within their historical context without making a priori aesthetic judgments.

In 1908, Sirén was appointed to the J.A. Berg Chair of Art History and Theory at the University of Stockholm, thus becoming the first professor of art history in Sweden, a distinction that attests both to his exceptionally early academic success and to the youth of the discipline as an independent university subject in the Nordic world. He held that chair until 1923, devoting himself with great systematic rigor to the study of medieval and Renaissance Italian painting. During this period, he produced fundamental contributions: in 1905, he proposed attributing a painting preserved at the Bandini Museum in Fiesole to the Florentine painter and illuminator Lorenzo Monaco, a proposal that ushered in a new era of studies on late Gothic Florentine art. He published studies on Giotto, on the early Tuscan and Pisan painters, and was among the first to attempt a systematic organization of the corpus of the Maestro di Tedice, the anonymous thirteenth-century Pisan painter whose stylistic identification Sirén proposed by grouping under that conventional name a coherent body of thirteenth-century works.

Sirén belongs to that extraordinary generation of art historians born between 1870 and 1890 who transformed the discipline: he corresponded with Lionello Venturi and shared with him an interest in medieval Tuscan painters and Leonardo. Like Venturi, Sirén was influenced by the aesthetic thought of Benedetto Croce, by Alois Riegl's Kunstwollen, the concept of "artistic will" as a key to interpreting style, and by Berenson's Kennerschaft. His intellectual circle also included Tancred Borenius and Onni Okkonen, other Finnish art historians working internationally with similar methodologies. He was also a member of the theosophical movement, of which he was among the first adherents in the Scandinavian region, and this spiritual sensitivity toward Eastern traditions likely paved the way for his subsequent turn to Chinese art.

After World War I, Sirén made the most surprising intellectual shift of his career: he gradually abandoned the history of Italian art to devote himself increasingly exclusively to Chinese art, a field that was virtually unexplored by Western historiography at the time. This change of course was not sudden: the scholarly method he had applied to Italian primitives, meticulous formal analysis, classification by hand, and the construction of attributive corpora, proved extraordinarily well suited to the study of a figurative tradition that was itself extremely rich and still largely unknown in the West. From 1928 to 1945, he served as Curator of Paintings and Sculptures at the Nationalmuseum in Stockholm, thus combining his research activities with a leading role in museum operations.

The fruits of Sirén's Orientalist period were of extraordinary magnitude. In 1924, he published The Walls and Gates of Peking, a documentary work on imperial Beijing.
