In 1926, Chinese Sculpture: Fifth to Fourteenth Centuries was published in five volumes, accompanied by an impressive collection of photographs. In 1929, he published A History of Early Chinese Painting, published by the Medici Society in London. In 1935, he published La scultura e la pittura cinesi in Italian for ISMEO in Rome. His magnum opus was Chinese Painting: Leading Masters and Principles (1950-1958), a seven-volume work that compiled, updated, and systematized decades of research, becoming the indispensable reference text for anyone studying Chinese painting in the English-speaking world. He also taught courses on Chinese art at Harvard University, bringing his research to the heart of American academia.

In 1956, Sirén was awarded the Charles Lang Freer Medal, the most prestigious American honor in the field of Asian art studies, presented by the Smithsonian Institution, an award that cemented his status as the world's foremost authority in the field. He died in Stockholm on June 26, 1966, at the age of eighty-seven, leaving a bibliographic legacy of rare breadth and quality.

Osvald Sirén stands out for his dual excellence: very few art historians have achieved leading positions in two fields as diverse as medieval Italian painting and Chinese art. For scholars of Italian medieval art, his contributions to fourteenth-century studies, on Pisan painted crosses, the Maestro di Tedice, Lorenzo Monaco, and fourteenth-century Florentine frescoes, remain historiographical reference points that are still discussed and cited. For the world of East Asian studies, his monumental work on Chinese painting still retains irreplaceable documentary value today, the result of decades of travel, access to private and public collections, and a systematic application of Western connoisseurship to a visual tradition of extraordinary complexity.

Stubblebine, James H.

1920 – 1987

James H. Stubblebine was an American art historian who specialized in early Italian painting, especially the Sienese school of the late thirteenth and early fourteenth centuries. He is regarded as one of the leading scholars of Italian Duecento and Trecento painting and made a major contribution to the institutional development of art history as a discipline in the United States.

Educated at Harvard University, where he graduated in 1942, Stubblebine became professor of art history at Rutgers University and was later named Distinguished Professor II. He helped build Rutgers' graduate program in art history and also taught at New York University's Institute of Fine Arts in the late 1970s and 1980s. His long-standing association with major American research institutions and museums, including the National Gallery of Art, anchored his work in both scholarly and curatorial practice.

Stubblebine's primary research focused on thirteenth- and fourteenth-century Italian panel painting, with particular emphasis on Duccio di Buoninsegna and his circle in Siena. His monumental two-volume study Duccio di Buoninsegna and His School combines iconographic, stylistic, and document-based analysis with an extensive photographic archive, reproducing essentially all of Duccio's surviving works and many by his contemporaries. He also published extensively on related figures such as Coppo di Marcovaldo and Guido da Siena, and his essays on Byzantine influence and the development of throne imagery in Duecento Tuscan painting remain frequently cited in debates on Italo-Byzantine transition.

For scholars working on twelfth- to fourteenth-century Italian painting and illuminated manuscripts, Stubblebine's work remains essential for understanding chronology, workshop practice, and stylistic evolution in late Duecento and early Trecento Siena. His method, combining meticulous formal analysis with documentary and archival research supported by systematic photographic evidence, continues to serve as a model for attributional and school-based studies in medieval Italian art.

Steinweg, Klara

Westphalia, 18 May 1903 - Meerbusch, 10 June 1972

Klara Steinweg was a German art historian specializing in Italian medieval and early Renaissance painting, best known for her decades-long collaboration with Richard Offner on the Critical and Historical Corpus of Florentine Painting - one of the most ambitious scholarly enterprises in the history of art historical studies. Although she worked largely in the shadow of her more celebrated partner, her contribution to the understanding of fourteenth-century Florentine painting was indispensable and lasting.

Steinweg was born on 18 May 1903 in Westphalia, Germany. The details of her early academic training are not fully documented in available sources, but her subsequent career demonstrates a thorough grounding in connoisseurship, archival methodology, and iconographic analysis. Like many German-trained art historians of her generation, she was shaped by the philological rigour that characterized the German Kunstwissenschaft tradition - a systematic, evidence-based approach to attributing and dating works of art.

The defining relationship of Steinweg's scholarly life was her partnership with Richard Offner (1889-1965), the American art historian whose A Critical and Historical Corpus of Florentine Painting aimed to catalogue and analyze, with exhaustive documentary precision, the entire body of Florentine painting from the twelfth century onwards. Steinweg became his primary collaborator from 1930 to 1965, contributing research, attribution analysis, and critical apparatus to multiple volumes. The project was published through the Institute of Fine Arts at New York University, and the volumes produced under their joint authorship covered pivotal figures of Trecento painting.

When Offner died in 1965, Steinweg assumed sole direction of the Corpus, continuing to produce volumes under the joint imprint of "Offner and Steinweg". Her contributions included landmark volumes on Jacopo di Cione (1965), Giovanni del Biondo (1967), and Niccolo di Pietro Gerini (1969). On attribution questions - such as the contested dating and authorship of major altarpieces by Orcagna and Jacopo di Cione - her positions were frequently cited and debated by subsequent scholars including Miklos Boskovits, Mirella Levi d'Ancona, and Carl Huter. Her final volume, on Andrea Bonaiuti, was published posthumously in 1979, seven years after her death.

Steinweg lived and worked in Florence for most of her adult life, immersing herself in the city whose artistic heritage she spent her career documenting. Her Florentine residence made her a central figure in the community of international art historians active in Italy during the mid-twentieth century. One of her research assistants in her final years was the young Patricia Rubin, who would go on to become a distinguished professor at the Institute of Fine Arts, NYU - a direct testimony to Steinweg's role as an informal teacher and mentor. She also had a significant influence on the formation of Miklos Boskovits, who made contact with her during his early career and who would later take over the direction of the Corpus after her death.

Steinweg's work was inseparable from the practice of photographic documentation that underpinned the Corpus. Her handwritten annotations on the backs of archival photographs - still preserved in the project's archives - record her first-hand observations of specific works, including notations made during direct inspection of paintings. This material evidence underlines how deeply her methodology depended on sustained, in-person confrontation with the objects of study, a practice central to the connoisseurship tradition she inherited from Offner.

Steinweg died on 10 June 1972, aged 69, in Meerbusch, in the Rhein-Kreis Neuss district of North Rhine-Westphalia, Germany - apparently having returned to her homeland at the very end of her life after decades in Florence. After her death, the Corpus was continued under the direction of Miklos Boskovits and Mina Gregori, a continuation that acknowledged the structural foundations she had laid. The Corpus of Florentine Painting remains, to this day, the essential reference for the study of Trecento Florentine painting, and Steinweg's name - paired with Offner's on volume after volume - ensures her permanent place in the historiography of Italian medieval art.

Strzygowski, Josef Rudolph Thomas

Biala (Kingdom of Galicia and Lodomeria), 7 March 1862 - Vienna, 2 January 1941

Josef Rudolph Thomas Strzygowski was a Polish-Austrian art historian and one of the most controversial and provocative scholars of his era, celebrated for radically expanding the geographical horizons of art history while simultaneously drawing condemnation for his ideologically compromised later work. He is remembered as a central figure of the Vienna School of Art History and, arguably, Europe's first professor of non-Western art history.

Strzygowski was born on March 7, 1862, in Biala, in the Kingdom of Galicia and Lodomeria (present-day Poland, then part of the Austrian Empire). His mother, Edle Trass von Friedelfeldt, came from minor nobility, while his father was a cloth manufacturer. Following family tradition, the young Strzygowski began an apprenticeship at a weaving plant in 1880, but abandoned this path in 1882 to enroll at the University of Vienna. He soon transferred to the University of Munich, where he immersed himself in art history and completed a dissertation on the iconography of the Baptism of Christ, published in 1885 under the title Ikonographie der Taufe Christi.

After completing his studies, Strzygowski spent three years in Rome, where he produced Cimabue und Rom (1887), an early and significant work that emphasized the Byzantine sources underlying the painting of Cimabue, a subject of direct relevance to the study of medieval Italian art. This work already signaled his lifelong conviction that the roots of Western European art lay not in Rome alone but in the broader Eastern Mediterranean world. He subsequently traveled to Thessaloniki, Mount Athos, Saint Petersburg, and Moscow, deepening his knowledge of Byzantine and Russian art.

In 1892, Strzygowski was appointed to the faculty of the University of Graz. Between 1894 and 1895, he lived in Cairo, where he studied early Byzantine and Islamic art in Egypt and compiled a landmark catalogue of Coptic art held in the Cairo Museum. This fieldwork positioned him as a pioneer in areas that were then largely unexplored by Western scholars, and his expertise soon attracted the attention of major institutions: Wilhelm von Bode, director of the Berlin Museums, enlisted Strzygowski to help build the Byzantine collection before the opening of the Kaiser-Friedrich Museum in 1904, expanding the holdings from fewer than 100 to approximately 2,000 objects.

The most intellectually explosive moment of Strzygowski's career came in 1901, with the publication of Orient oder Rom: Beitrage zur Geschichte der spatantiken und fruhchristlichen Kunst ("The Orient or Rome: Contributions to the History of Late Antique and Early Christian Art"). In this polemical work, he directly challenged the dominant paradigm that Rome was the fountainhead of early Christian and medieval art, arguing instead that the Near East, particularly Syria, Mesopotamia, Armenia, and Egypt, played the decisive formative role. This thesis shook the foundations of the discipline and ignited a scholarly debate that resonated for decades.

Upon the death of Franz Wickhoff, Strzygowski was appointed in 1909 to his successor's chair at the University of Vienna, a position secured in part through the breadth of his research and, reportedly, the advocacy of Archduke Franz Ferdinand. From this prestigious platform, he continued to publish prolifically on Byzantium, Islam, Armenia, Norse art, and Slavic visual culture, developing a sweeping, non-Eurocentric theoretical framework for art history that was radical for its time.

His two-volume Die Baukunst der Armenier und Europa (1918), "The Architecture of the Armenians and Europe," advanced the daring claim that the origins of Gothic architecture could be traced to Armenian building traditions, a thesis that was largely rejected by subsequent scholarship. He also entered a notable controversy with the archaeologist Ernst Herzfeld over the origins of the Mshatta facade; in this dispute, Herzfeld's position was ultimately vindicated.

Strzygowski retired from the University of Vienna in 1933 and founded the Gesellschaft fur vergleichende Kunstforschung ("Society for Comparative Art History") in 1934 to propagate his theoretical views. Tragically, in his final years he embraced the nationalist and racist ideology of National Socialism, a drift that scholars have interpreted not merely as political opportunism but as the disturbing logical conclusion of the ethnic and racial categories that had gradually infiltrated his art historical thinking.

He died on January 2, 1941, in Vienna. His private library was bequeathed to the Art History Department of the University of Vienna, where it remains.

Despite his methodological inconsistencies and the deep ideological stain on his later work, Strzygowski's intellectual legacy is complex and cannot be dismissed outright. His insistence on looking beyond the Greco-Roman world helped legitimize Islamic art, Coptic art, and non-Western visual traditions as serious fields of academic inquiry. Several of his most gifted students, most notably Otto Demus and Fritz Novotny, successfully pursued these broadened horizons without adopting their teacher's ideology. Today, in an era of global art history and decolonized museum practice, Strzygowski's restless geographical curiosity finds a kind of posthumous vindication, even as his racial theorizing remains a cautionary chapter in the history of the discipline.

T

Tambini Strocchi, Anna

c. 1942 - 2025

Anna Tambini Strocchi was one of the most authoritative Italian scholars of medieval and Renaissance art in the Romagna region, particularly celebrated for her pioneering studies on the Giottesque School of Rimini and on manuscript illumination between the thirteenth and fifteenth centuries. She died at the age of 82, mourned by the cultural community of Faenza and beyond.

Tambini studied Art History at the Università di Bologna under the guidance of Carlo Volpe, obtaining her Diploma di perfezionamento in Storia dell'Arte in 1979. Her graduation thesis became the foundation for her first major book, Pittura dall'Alto Medioevo al Tardogotico nel territorio di Faenza e Forli (Faenza, 1982), which established her as a rigorous and original voice in the study of Romagnole art history.

Her primary field was the art of the Middle Ages and Renaissance in Romagna, though she also devoted significant attention to eighteenth-century painting. Among her most appreciated contributions are studies on the Giottesque Riminese School, on Forli's medieval painting tradition, on Late Gothic art in Romagna, and on manuscript illumination in Romagna from the Duecento to the Quattrocento.

Over the course of her career, Tambini produced more than one hundred publications in specialized journals, including international venues. She contributed to the catalogues of the Pinacoteca di Ferrara and the Pinacoteca di Bologna, and she curated the medieval and Renaissance painting section in the catalogue of the Pinacoteca Comunale di Ravenna (2001). She also wrote entries for the Enciclopedia Treccani and the German Kunstler Lexikon.

Her most ambitious publishing project was a three-volume series on the history of figurative arts in Faenza: Le origini (2006), Il Gotico (2007), and Il Rinascimento (2009), followed by a contribution on Faentine painters of the first half of the sixteenth century in a fourth volume. These works, together with her cataloguing activity at the Biblioteca di Faenza and her contributions to the five-volume series on Faenza's historic neighborhoods edited by Lorenzo Savelli, form a major scholarly corpus on local art history.

In 2001 she also co-authored with Morelli and Tabanelli Quella chiesa nata dalle acque. L'oratorio di Croce Coperta a Lugo, a focused study on a lesser-known but artistically significant oratory in the Ravenna province.

Tambini lectured at the universities of Trieste and Urbino and supervised theses in both institutions. She presented papers at symposia in Mercatello, Tolentino, Vignola, Camerino, and Urbino, reflecting the breadth of her recognized authority across central Italy. In 2021 she received the honorary distinction Faentina Lontana, conferred by the city of Faenza.

Her legacy is that of a meticulous and passionate researcher who dedicated her scholarly life to recovering and illuminating an artistic heritage that might otherwise have remained largely forgotten.

Tartuferi, Angelo

Florence, 16 August 1957

Angelo Tartuferi is one of Italy's most authoritative art historians in the field of medieval and early Renaissance Tuscan painting, with a particular focus on the period from the thirteenth to the fifteenth century. His scholarship combines connoisseurship, archival discipline, and museum practice, and has made him a major reference point in studies of Florentine painting between the Duecento and the Quattrocento.

He graduated with honors in Art History at the University of Florence in 1982 under Mina Gregori, the same teacher as Giordana Benazzi. He later earned his Specialization Diploma at La Sapienza University of Rome (1986), followed by a PhD in 1989 with a dissertation titled La pittura a Firenze nel Duecento. Decisive to his formation was also his collaboration with Miklós Boskovits, a leading scholar of medieval Florentine painting at the Germanic Institute of Art History in Florence, an experience he has described as a true scholarly bottega in the most concrete and demanding sense.

On 25 July 1990 he entered the Italian Ministry for Cultural Heritage as an art-historical official. He served as Deputy Director of the Galleria dell'Accademia in Florence, then moved in 2006 to the Uffizi as Director of the Department of Duecento-to-Quattrocento painting. Between 2009 and 2013 he also held the position of Deputy Director of the Uffizi. In 2013, after the retirement of Franca Falletti, he became Director of the Galleria dell'Accademia, a role he held until the 2015 ministerial reform. From 2016 to 2020 he directed the department dedicated to Michelangelesque sculpture and Duecento-to-Quattrocento painting at the same museum. From 2020 to 2024 he served as Director of the Museo di San Marco in Florence, where he oversaw, among other projects, the new installation of the room dedicated to Fra Angelico.

Tartuferi has authored numerous monographs, essays, and articles in leading journals, including Paragone Arte, Arte Cristiana, Antichità Viva, Commentari d'arte, Revue de l'Art, and Studi di Storia dell'Arte. His research spans Duecento Florentine painting, late Gothic culture, and the early Renaissance, with special attention to attributional questions and to the reconstruction of minor or anonymous artistic personalities. He obtained the Italian National Scientific Qualification in 2012 for associate-professor rank and in 2020 for full-professor rank (sector 10/B1, Art History), and he has taught university courses at the University of Perugia and other institutions.

His scholarly stature has been recognized by a collective Festschrift, Scritti di Storia dell'Arte in his honor, published by Campisano Editore and featuring contributions from numerous international scholars on medieval and early Renaissance topics. After retiring in August 2024, he has continued to work as an independent researcher and exhibition curator.

Thode, Heinrich

Dresden, 13 January 1857 - Copenhagen, 19 November 1920

Heinrich Thode was a German art historian, renowned for his studies on the Italian Renaissance and especially on Saint Francis of Assisi, and for his role in shaping the discipline of art history in late nineteenth- and early twentieth-century Germany. He combined rigorous archival research with a strongly personal, often romantic vision of Italy and of the development of modern individuality out of medieval spirituality.

Thode was born in Dresden in 1857 into a respected North German bourgeois family. His father, Robert Thode (1825-1898), was a banker who had founded the "Dresdener Bank Robert Thode & Co." in Dresden, later incorporated into the Dresdner Bank in 1891, which placed the family in a comfortable financial and social position. His mother, Adolfine Dzondi (1822-1900), was the daughter of a Saxon Lutheran pastor, and Thode grew up as the third of four children in a protected, cultivated milieu where Protestant religiosity and bourgeois culture intersected.

From an early age he received a solid humanistic education: after the Gymnasium at Gorlitz he moved in the typical fashion of the German Bildungsburger between schools and university cities, building the classical and philological foundation that would later support his art-historical work. This combination of banking-bourgeois pragmatism and clerical-intellectual background helps explain both his practical engagement in institutional life and his persistent preoccupation with religious questions in art.

Thode initially enrolled in law at the University of Leipzig, following a conventional path for a young man of his class. However, he soon turned away from jurisprudence and, after being sent to Vienna, discovered art history, then still a relatively young academic discipline, which he continued to study in Berlin and Munich. In Vienna he wrote his doctoral dissertation under the guidance of Moritz Thausing, completing it in 1880 and thus entering the circle of the great positivist-philological school of Central European art history.

In 1886 he obtained his Habilitation at the University of Bonn as Privatdozent in art history, which gave him the formal right to teach at the university. These years of formation placed him at the crossroads of several methodological traditions: the documentary, source-critical Viennese school; the museum-based connoisseurship associated with figures like Wilhelm von Bode; and, increasingly, an idealist interest in the spiritual and cultural "sense" of historical periods. This plural background would mark his later work, which moves continually between archival precision and broader cultural interpretation.

In 1886 Thode married Daniela von Bulow (1860-1940), whom he had met in Venice during a study trip to Italy. Daniela was the daughter of Cosima Liszt (later Cosima Wagner) and of her first husband, the conductor Hans von Bulow, and thus part of the circle surrounding Richard Wagner and the Bayreuth enterprise. Through this marriage Thode entered the complex world of late nineteenth-century German musical and cultural elites, tied to the cult of Wagner and to a network of artists, intellectuals and patrons.

This connection to Bayreuth and to Cosima's family reinforced Thode's interest in the ideological and spiritual dimensions of art, since the Wagnerian milieu cultivated an almost religious conception of the artist and of the artwork as vehicles of a new Germanic spirituality. Socially, the marriage placed him in the border zone between academic art history and broader cultural debates, a position that would later shape his strong, sometimes polemical interventions on contemporary German art and on the threat he perceived in French Impressionism.

In 1889, thanks to the recommendation of Wilhelm von Bode, Thode was appointed director of the Stadelsches Kunstinstitut in Frankfurt am Main. This prestigious museum-directorship placed him at the heart of German museum practice at a time when museums were becoming key instruments for the formation of national taste. During his Frankfurt years he met the painter Hans Thoma, with whom he established a long friendship, indicative of his closeness to certain strands of German naturalism and of his appreciation for contemporary artists who, in his view, remained faithful to a specifically German sensibility.

However, disagreements with the museum administration led Thode to resign in October 1891, only two years after his appointment. The conflict reflected both institutional tensions and Thode's strong personality: he was not simply a curator but an intellectual with pronounced ideas about what a museum should collect and how it should present art to the public. His departure from Frankfurt marked a shift from primarily curatorial work back toward the university and gave him greater freedom to develop his own interpretive projects.

In 1893 Thode was appointed extraordinarius (associate professor) of art history at the University of Heidelberg, and in 1896 he became ordinarius, the first full professor of art history at that university. This position placed him among the central figures of German academic art history, a generation that included scholars who were institutionalizing the field within the university system. At Heidelberg he built a thriving art-historical seminar, training students in both archival research and close formal analysis, while integrating literary and theological materials into his lectures.

His teaching at Heidelberg reflected his dual interest in Italian Renaissance art and in the contemporary German artistic scene. On the one hand he offered historic courses on Michelangelo, the Renaissance, and the spiritual origins of modern art; on the other he took clear positions in the debates about what constituted "true" German art at the end of the nineteenth century. In this way he helped shape both the historical canon and the taste of a generation of educated German viewers.

Beyond the university, Thode nourished an intense personal and intellectual relationship with Italy. He settled for long periods on Lake Garda, where he acquired and developed a villa that became an "officina della maturita," a workshop of his mature years, filled with books, artworks, and memories of ancient and Renaissance art. This villa, later to become Gabriele D'Annunzio's Vittoriale degli Italiani, preserved many traces of Thode's presence and of the art-historical and literary work he carried out there.

In this Italian retreat he constructed something like a hortus conclusus, a closed garden of study and contemplation, embodying both his love of Renaissance culture and his romantic, almost Goethean idea of a fusion between scholarly research and the aesthetic experience of place. The Italian critic Silvia Urbini has highlighted how Thode's essays and Kunstnovellen (art novellas), written in this context, belong to a tradition of "romantic science" where documentary and philological research intertwine with narrative forms. For Thode, Italy was not merely an object of study but a lived environment that shaped his historiographical imagination.

Thode's most famous work is his study on Saint Francis and the origins of Renaissance art: Franz von Assisi und die Anfange der Kunst der Renaissance in Italien, first published in 1885. In this book he argued that the Renaissance should be understood as a process of emancipation from medieval values in which the modern individual emerges, yet in his view this emancipation remains deeply marked by the spirituality of Saint Francis of Assisi. The work was widely read, translated (for example into French and later into Italian), and helped consolidate the idea of Franciscan spirituality as a crucial factor in the birth of Renaissance art.

Alongside this seminal study, Thode devoted important monographs and essays to Michelangelo and to various aspects of Italian Renaissance art, confirming his reputation as a specialist in this period. At the same time, he wrote extensively on German art of the late nineteenth century, championing what he regarded as "great German art," characterized by technical skill, realism and a distinct national spirit, and sharply opposing the dominance of French Impressionism and other foreign currents. He insisted that art should be intelligible to the broader public, not only to academics and the bourgeois elite, which aligned his scholarship with broader debates on culture and nation.

In his writings on modern art, Thode expressed strong criticism of Impressionism and of what he perceived as a cosmopolitan trend undermining specifically German artistic traditions. He accused modern French art of attempting to destroy both "true" German art and the "true" German spirit, formulating an opposition between rooted national realism and foreign, decadent experimentation. These positions, while part of broader contemporary debates, were later regarded as important antecedents for the cultural policies of the Third Reich, particularly the condemnation of so-called "degenerate art."

This retrospective association has complicated Thode's reputation, as his work is now seen both as a significant contribution to the historical understanding of the Renaissance and as a source of ideas that could be instrumentalized for nationalist and exclusionary cultural politics. Modern scholarship, such as the monographic study Henry Thode (1857-1920): Leben und Werk, has sought to reassess his oeuvre in its historical context, emphasizing his role as the first full professor of art history at Heidelberg and as a key figure in the institutional consolidation of the discipline, while also critically examining the ideological dimensions of his thought.

Thode's personal life became increasingly unsettled in the years surrounding the First World War. His strong German nationalism and his Italian ties, combined with the political upheavals of the period, contributed to a sense of displacement; he and his wife moved around Germany until 1919, eventually leaving the country. In 1919 they settled in Copenhagen, where Thode sought a degree of peace after the traumas of war and professional conflict.

He died in Copenhagen in November 1920, reportedly depressed following complications from a gastric operation. His death in relative exile, away from both his native Dresden and his beloved Lake Garda, gives his biography a somewhat tragic cadence, contrasting with the secure, bourgeois beginnings of his life. Yet his writings continued to circulate, especially his work on Saint Francis and the Renaissance, which remained a reference point for discussions on the spiritual origins of modern art well into the twentieth century.

Thode stands as a representative of a generation of art historians who experienced and theorized a "time of upheaval" in art and culture, bridging the nineteenth and twentieth centuries. As the first ordinarius for art history at Heidelberg, he played a central role in institutionalizing the subject within German universities and in forming a generation of students who would continue to develop the discipline. His work on Saint Francis, the Renaissance, and Michelangelo contributed to the canonization of certain narratives about the birth of modern individuality and the relationship between medieval spirituality and Renaissance art, narratives that continue to interest scholars today.

At the same time, his conservative cultural politics, his hostility to Impressionism, and his insistence on an essentialized "German spirit" mark him as a figure in whom scholarly insight and ideological rigidity coexist in tension. Contemporary historians of art and culture read Thode not only for his factual contributions but also as a case study in how art history can participate in wider national and ideological projects. This dual legacy-scholarly and ideological-makes him a particularly instructive figure for understanding the history of art history as a discipline.

Todini, Filippo

Rome, 1957–

Italian art historian best known for his studies on Umbrian painting and the painting schools of Central Italy between the thirteenth and sixteenth centuries. Todini specializes in Italian art history with a particular focus on Umbria and on the artistic dynamics between Bologna, Perugia, and Florence from the late Middle Ages to the modern era. His work is frequently cited in studies on minor painters, anonymous masters, and attributional problems, and he is regarded as an authority on the cataloging and reconstruction of Perugian and Umbrian workshops.

Among his best-known works is the two-volume set La pittura umbra dal Duecento al primo Cinquecento (1989), which functions as a large-scale critical survey and catalogue-style reference for Umbrian painting. He has also edited volumes such as Studi di storia dell'arte (conference proceedings and research collections) and has published studies on seventeenth-century Umbrian painting, continuing his analysis of local history, patronage structures, and workshop formation.

Todini has repeatedly intervened in attribution debates, proposing or confirming identities of "anonymous masters" and reattributing works to lesser-known painters or collaborative workshop contexts. His criteria are often cited in articles and exhibition catalogues for their close attention to stylistic details, internal workshop chronology, and the circulation of forms between Umbria and Tuscany.

Toesca, Pietro

Pietra Ligure, 12 July 1877 – Rome, 9 March 1962

Giovanni Pietro Toesca was one of the greatest Italian medieval art historians of the twentieth century, whose work laid the methodological foundations of the discipline for generations of scholars. Born in Pietra Ligure, Liguria, to a noble family originally from Saorgio, he was orphaned at the age of twelve. He died in Rome on 9 March 1962 after dedicating over six decades to research, philological cataloging, and the critical evaluation of a vast body of artworks.

Toesca was trained in Rome under the guidance of Adolfo Venturi, the patriarch of Italian art history, of whom he became the most worthy successor in the teaching of medieval and modern art history. In 1905, he began his career as a lecturer at the Scientific-Literary Academy of Milan, and in 1907 he was appointed to the newly established chair of Art History at the University of Turin. These Turin years were extraordinarily productive: in 1908 he published a seminal essay in L'Arte on fourteenth- and fifteenth-century Italian gold-leaf stained glass with graffiti, and in 1909 he identified the illuminator Francesco Marmitta as the creator of Domenico Della Rovere's Missal.

In 1912, La pittura e la miniatura nella Lombardia fino alla metà del Quattrocento was published, a work that represented the first systematic attempt to reconstruct the landscape of Lombard figurative art from the Middle Ages onward and established its significance on a European scale. From May 1912 to December 1913, he also served on the Board of Directors of the Civic Museum of Turin, strengthening his ties with the city's cultural institutions.

In 1913, the first issue of the monumental Storia dell'arte italiana was published. Its first volume, Il Medioevo, would be completed in 1927 after years of uninterrupted research. The work became a central reference for the study of Italian medieval art and remained one of the most influential syntheses in twentieth-century historiography.

In 1914, Toesca moved to Florence, where he formed a deep intellectual friendship with Bernard Berenson, sharing the method of direct stylistic analysis of works. This Florentine phase strengthened his confidence in saper vedere, the ability to observe the distinctive stylistic characteristics of a work directly, as an indispensable foundation of art history. In 1926, he moved permanently to Rome, where he succeeded Venturi as professor of Medieval and Modern Art History at La Sapienza, concluding his teaching career in 1948.

Toesca's method was based on rigorous philological analysis of styles, combined with close visual observation. He theorized this approach in two articles published in the Annali della istruzione media: Una lezione su Giotto (1929) and Saper vedere (1932). In these texts, rare examples of explicit methodological reflection, he argued that understanding the distinctive stylistic qualities of a work constitutes the true foundation of the discipline.

He also made continuous and thoughtful use of photography as a tool for analyzing and documenting artworks, anticipating practices that would later become standard in art-historical research. This integration of direct viewing, philology, and visual documentation was one of the strongest features of his method.

In addition to university teaching, Toesca held institutional roles of the highest order. From 1929 to 1937, he served as director of the History of Medieval and Modern Art section of the Treccani Enciclopedia Italiana, leaving a lasting methodological imprint on the treatment of medieval artists. In 1939, he joined the Technical Council of the Central Institute for Restoration, helping to define scientific criteria for restoration in Italy.

From 1944 to 1952, he served as president of the National Institute of Archaeology and Art History (INASA) in Rome, guiding the institution through the difficult postwar years and returning it to its headquarters at Palazzo Venezia in 1945. His chair in Rome was also an extraordinary breeding ground for talent.

Among his most illustrious students were Roberto Longhi, Federico Zeri, Giulio Carlo Argan, Ernst Kitzinger, Carlo Bertelli, Sergio Bettini, Ferdinando Bologna, and Giovanni Carandente. Roberto Longhi, destined to become the most influential art critic of postwar Italy, took his first steps under Toesca's guidance, though he later developed a more dynamic and literary critical approach than his mentor's philological sobriety.

Toesca's definitive masterpiece remains the two-volume Storia dell'arte italiana. The first volume, Il Medioevo (1913-1927), covers Italian art from its Christian origins through the thirteenth century, while the second, Il Trecento (1951), resumes the discussion from the age of Giotto and Duccio. Also central are Monumenti e studi per la storia della miniatura italiana (1929) and La pittura fiorentina del Trecento (1929), testifying to his specific and lasting influence on the study of manuscript illumination and Trecento painting.

Toesca married Elena Berti (1900-1967), also an art historian and author of numerous essays; they had one daughter, Ilaria Toesca, who followed in her parents' footsteps. From 1946, he was a national member of the Accademia dei Lincei, the highest academic honor in Italy. Critics have described him as an example of exemplary moral consistency as both a teacher and a historian, qualities that made him not only a rigorous scholar but also a master in the fullest sense of the word.

Torriti, Piero

Montisi, 1924 – Siena, 2015

Italian art historian, critic, and museum professional who played a central role in the study and stewardship of Sienese art throughout the second half of the twentieth century. Born in the hill town of Montisi in the Sienese countryside, he developed an enduring sensitivity to local artistic traditions and devotional imagery. He studied art history at the University of Florence under Mario Salmi, receiving a formation grounded in connoisseurship, contextual analysis, and the critical use of archival sources. Entering the state art-heritage administration after graduation, he eventually became Superintendent of the Soprintendenza per i Beni Artistici e Storici for the provinces of Siena and Grosseto, a role that placed him at the intersection of conservation policy, museum management, and scholarly research. As a scholar, Torriti focused especially on Sienese painting and sculpture from the Duecento to the early modern period, studying artists such as Duccio, Jacopo della Quercia, and the Lorenzetti circle, while also addressing the devotional culture, architecture, and urban image of Siena and its territory, including Pienza and the impact of the Renaissance papacy on local commissions. He curated important exhibitions and restorations, edited catalogues that combined archival documentation, technical analysis, and iconographic discussion, and wrote widely used books and guides such as Siena, storia e capolavori and Arte a Siena, making Sienese art accessible to a broad public without sacrificing scholarly precision. Through his work as superintendent, scholar, and popularizer, he helped define the modern understanding of Sienese art and left a lasting legacy in both academic and museum circles.

Toscano, Bruno

Spoleto, 9 April 1930-

Bruno Toscano is one of the leading Italian art historians of the second half of the twentieth century, as well as a painter, and he has played a fundamental role in the study and preservation of historical and artistic heritage, particularly in Umbria and Italy.

Born in Spoleto in 1930, Toscano initially trained as a painter and, beginning in the 1950s, participated in exhibitions in various Italian cities, joining the so-called "Spoleto Group" within the Informal art movement. He was among the promoters of the "Spoleto Prize" (1953-1963), which helped make the city one of the most vibrant centers of postwar contemporary art, fostering a close dialogue between artistic practice and historical-critical reflection. Parallel to his early work as a painter, he enrolled in the Faculty of Letters at La Sapienza University in Rome and in 1952 published his first study on fourteenth-century Umbrian painting, an early sign of a structural interest in medieval art.

After his formative years, Toscano embarked on a long academic career, becoming a full professor of the History of Art Criticism at the Faculty of Education at La Sapienza and later of the History of Modern Art at the same university. From 1992 to 2002, he taught Modern Art History at Roma Tre University, where he directed the Department of Historical-Artistic, Archaeological, and Conservation Studies, helping to define a strongly interdisciplinary approach to heritage studies. Institutional recognition of his work is reflected in the President of the Republic's Gold Medal for Cultural Merit (2003) and in his appointment, in 2016, as a member of the Scientific Committee of the National Gallery of Umbria.

Toscano's extensive and multifaceted scholarly output spans medieval, modern, and contemporary art history, with studies published in Italian and international books and journals. One of the cornerstones of his work is the definition of criteria for a systematic understanding of Italy's historical and artistic heritage, including not only preserved works but also those lost or stolen, and emphasizing the relationship between artists, patrons, and local contexts. His research on the relationship between museums and their local contexts and on artistic geography aims to reconcile the formal autonomy of the artwork with the multiple cultural, social, and material contexts in which it is created and transmitted, making art history an explicitly critical and problematic discipline.

Alongside art-historical research in the strict sense, Toscano has dedicated numerous contributions to restoration, museology, and heritage policy. In programmatic texts and in works such as the Vademecum for an Art History That Does Not Exist, he proposes tools for interpreting the Italian reality based on awareness of the gaps, dispersions, and stratifications of heritage, calling for the systematic integration of documentation, works in situ, and museum collections. In this context, the museum is never conceived as an isolated entity, but as a node in a network of relationships with the territory, urban and rural memory, and cultural landscapes, particularly in a "laboratory region" such as Umbria.

The relationship with Umbria, his homeland, is a constant in both his artistic practice and his historical-critical reflection, from the pioneering Spoleto in pietre (1963) to his most recent interventions on the city and landscape. Toscano is often presented as a leading figure in the awareness of Umbrian heritage, capable of combining cultural activism, scientific research, and educational activities, and of engaging with local authorities, heritage agencies, and museum institutions. His participation, as a member of the scientific committee, at the National Gallery of Umbria reaffirms this role as a mediator between local communities and the national historical-artistic tradition.

Although he became best known as an art historian, Toscano did not abandon painting, as evidenced by his watercolor exhibitions from 2013 to 2018, accompanied by catalogues that intertwine personal memory, landscape observation, and a critical awareness of the figurative tradition. In these works, that dual identity of artist and scholar emerges, in which the problems of seeing, of constructing space, and of the relationship with historical time are put to the test directly in practice. The same attention to the distinction and dialogue between the preserved and the lost, central to his studies, becomes in painting a meditation on the fragment, on urban or natural detail, and on light, in continuity with his long-standing reflection on Umbrian landscapes.

Tronzo, William

active late 20th-21st century

William Tronzo received his Ph.D. in the History of Art and Architecture from Harvard University (1973-1982) and his undergraduate degree in History from Haverford College (1969-1973). After completing his doctorate he taught at Johns Hopkins University, Duke University, and Tulane University before settling at the University of California, San Diego (UCSD), where he currently holds the position of Senior Teaching Professor in the History of Art, Architecture and Landscape, and serves as Director of European Studies.

Tronzo has held research fellowships and appointments at many of the most prestigious institutions in art-historical scholarship, including the American Academy in Rome, Dumbarton Oaks in Washington, the Institute for Advanced Study in Princeton, the Center for Advanced Study in the Visual Arts (CASVA) at the National Gallery of Art, the Bibliotheca Hertziana in Rome, the École des hautes études en sciences sociales in Paris, the Huntington Library, the Clark Art Institute, and the Stanford Humanities Center. This range of affiliations reflects the interdisciplinary breadth of his work across Byzantine studies, medieval Latin history, Islamic art history, and Renaissance studies.

He is also a member of the Collegio dei Docenti del Dottorato di Ricerca in the Department of Humanistic Studies at Università degli Studi Roma Tre, consolidating his ongoing connection to Italian academic life.

Tronzo's most celebrated and controversial contribution to medieval art history is his monograph The Cultures of His Kingdom: Roger II and the Cappella Palatina in Palermo (Princeton University Press, 1997). The study advanced a major reinterpretation of the Cappella Palatina, arguing that the building in its present form reflects two distinct planning projects with divergent aims in the twelfth century, rather than a single unified program of multicultural display. This thesis generated sustained debate because it challenged the long-dominant narrative of Norman Sicilian harmony and instead emphasized political tension and competing ideological agendas in the chapel's formation. The book remains a standard reference for scholarship on the monument.

His interpretation emphasizes not only decoration, but also patronage process: who commissioned what, in what sequence, and for what political purpose. This focus reflects his broader interest in the relationship between visual culture and power in the medieval world.

Beyond the Cappella Palatina, Tronzo has pursued sustained research on what he calls the hybrid culture of the Italian South, the multicultural civilization of Sicily and southern Italy from the Norman period through the court of Frederick II and into the early Renaissance. He has co-directed major projects on buildings, cities, and landscapes of the later medieval Italian South funded by the National Endowment for the Humanities (NEH), as well as a three-year program of research, seminars, and conferences on the medieval Mediterranean at the American Academy in Rome, funded by the Getty Foundation.

His monograph Petrarch's Two Gardens: Landscape and the Image of Movement (Italica Press, 2014) extends this inquiry into the early Renaissance, with a significant opening chapter on La Zisa and La Cuba in Palermo, surviving Fatimid-Norman garden pavilions of the twelfth century. Through these sites, Tronzo explores landscape as a social network and as a mediator of images. The study illustrates his characteristic method: beginning with a specific object or place to open broader questions of cultural exchange, representation, and the movement of ideas across time and space.

He has also published extensively on historiography and method in art history, examining how the discipline constructs narrative and what assumptions underlie scholarly judgment. For research on the art of the twelfth-century Mediterranean, including Monreale and the Cappella Palatina, Fatimid elements in Norman art, and the interplay of Byzantine, Arab, and Latin visual traditions, Tronzo is essential reading. If Jeremy Johns approaches Norman Sicily primarily through documentary and archaeological evidence grounded in Islamic studies, Tronzo approaches it through formal analysis and the interpretation of buildings and images; together, their work offers complementary lenses on Roger II's kingdom and its artistic legacy.

V

Van Marle, Raimond

The Hague, 1887 – Perugia, 18 November 1936

Valentin Raimond Silvain van Marle was a Dutch art historian of remarkable versatility and ambition, best remembered today as the author of the monumental The Development of the Italian Schools of Painting - a work that remains one of the most comprehensive surveys of Italian painting ever attempted. Though largely self-funded and working outside the formal academy, he produced a body of scholarship that still serves as a fundamental reference for anyone studying medieval and Renaissance Italian art.

Van Marle was born in The Hague in 1887 to a family of mixed European origins: his mother was Hungarian and his father was of distant Picard descent, which explains the French-sounding surname. In 1906, at the age of nineteen, he moved to Paris to pursue higher studies, enrolling at two of France's most prestigious scholarly institutions - the Ecole des Chartes, renowned for its training in the analysis of historical documents and manuscripts, and the Ecole Pratique des Hautes Etudes. In 1910 he was awarded a doctorate from the Sorbonne, a considerable intellectual achievement that shaped his rigorous, source-based approach to historical inquiry.

His first published works were not about Italian art but about medieval Northern European history and philosophy. In 1908 - while still a student - he published Le Comte de Hollande sous Philippe le Bon, a study of the County of Holland under Philip the Good of Burgundy. In 1916 he produced a Dutch-language monograph on the German mystic Meister Eckhart, titled De mystieke leer van Meister Eckhart, demonstrating his command of both religious thought and vernacular scholarship. These early works revealed a scholar equally at ease with political history, mystical theology, and archival research.

The decisive turning point in van Marle's life came in 1918, when, following his marriage, he relocated to a villa in the San Marco district of Perugia, in the heart of Umbria. Blessed with a private income that freed him from institutional obligations, he committed himself entirely to the study of Italian painting. Perugia was an ideal base: surrounded by Umbrian altarpieces, frescoes, and the artistic heritage of central Italy, he could conduct direct visual research while corresponding with scholars across Europe. He began contributing regularly to the Bollettino d'Arte and other international specialist journals, establishing himself as a serious voice in the growing field of Italian art history.

Van Marle's supreme achievement was the encyclopaedic The Development of the Italian Schools of Painting, a project of almost inconceivable scope covering Italian painting from the Romanesque era to the end of the fifteenth century. Published between 1923 and 1938 in Dutch, French, English, and Italian, the work was originally planned in 21 volumes, though van Marle lived to complete only 17 of them. After his death in 1936, the art historian Frederick Mason Perkins and his widow Charlotte van Marle continued the work, completing the 19th volume and the general index. The first two volumes were subsequently translated into Italian by Alba Buitoni in 1938. The series remains a touchstone for scholars of medieval and early Renaissance Italian painting, offering detailed artist-by-artist and school-by-school analysis that has not been fully superseded.

Beyond Italian painting, van Marle turned his attention to the broader visual culture of the medieval period. His two-volume Iconographie de l'art profane au Moyen-Age et a la Renaissance, et la decoration des demeures (1931-1932) explored secular iconography - the imagery of everyday life, allegory, and decoration that adorned private dwellings alongside the more thoroughly studied religious art of the period. This work demonstrated his sensitivity to sociological dimensions of art history, treating images not merely as aesthetic objects but as documents of medieval and Renaissance culture and society.

Van Marle was also a collector of photographic documentation, and his personal photo library became part of a distinguished archival tradition. His collection is preserved today at the Fondazione Giorgio Cini in Venice, alongside the photographic archives of scholars such as Evelyn Sandberg Vavala - a testament to the importance of visual documentation in early twentieth-century art historical method.

Van Marle was part of an international network of art historians active in Italy during the interwar period. His name appears in the correspondence archives related to Belle da Costa Greene, the celebrated director of the Morgan Library, connecting him to the transatlantic circuits of medieval manuscript scholarship and connoisseurship. His collaboration with Frederick Mason Perkins - himself a specialist in Sienese and Umbrian painting - further anchored him within the community of foreign scholars who made central Italy their intellectual home.

Van Marle died in Perugia on 18 November 1936, with two further manuscripts still in preparation. The political climate of Fascist Italy cast a shadow even over his burial: the regime's hostility toward foreign scholars meant that he could not be interred in the city where he had lived and worked for nearly two decades. Instead, he was buried in the remote village of Pozzale, a district of Pieve di Cadore in the Dolomites - a poignant geographical displacement that reflects the precarious position of cosmopolitan scholarship under nationalist politics. His work was nonetheless reissued in the postwar period, with Hacker Art Books producing a reprint edition as late as 1970, confirming the enduring utility of his surveys for generations of art historians.

Valagussa, Giovanni Francesco Maria

Milan, 1963 –

Giovanni Francesco Maria Valagussa (born 1963, Milan) is one of Italy's most distinguished art historians specializing in medieval and Renaissance painting, best known for his long tenure as curator of the Accademia Carrara in Bergamo and his fundamental contributions to the study of Italian Trecento and Quattrocento art.

Valagussa graduated in Lettere with a specialization in Art History from the Università Cattolica del Sacro Cuore in Milan, where the eminent Hungarian-Italian medievalist Miklos Boskovits served as co-supervisor of his thesis. This formative relationship with Boskovits, one of the great connoisseurs of Italian medieval painting, would leave a lasting imprint on his scholarly approach. He then deepened his training at the prestigious Fondazione Roberto Longhi in Florence, studying under Mina Gregori, and subsequently completed his PhD at the Università degli Studi di Torino under the guidance of Giovanni Romano. His educational path from 1982 to 1994 thus united three of Italy's most rigorous schools of art historical connoisseurship.

After a brief period teaching in secondary schools in Milan following his graduation, Valagussa entered the museum world by winning a competitive examination for the post of conservatore at the Museo Civico Ala Ponzone in Cremona (1998-1999), an experience he did not find entirely fulfilling. He subsequently held the role of conservator at the Accademia Tadini in Lovere (2000-2001), a small but significant institution on Lake Iseo, where he worked with great satisfaction. Simultaneously, beginning in 1998, he began teaching regularly at the Università Cattolica, both in Milan and in Brescia, offering courses in art history and museology that he has continued to this day.

In 2001, Valagussa won the competitive selection for the position of conservator at the Accademia Carrara in Bergamo, a contest promoted by the then-director Francesco Rossi. He would remain in that role for over twenty years, until April 2022, becoming the institution's most enduring scholarly figure of the modern era. During the museum's lengthy closure for renovation (2008-2015), he was the principal curator of the Accademia Carrara's travelling exhibitions, bringing the collection to Lausanne, Rome, Brussels, Canberra, and Moscow, among other international venues. He also co-curated with Simone Facchinetti important publications on the collection, including catalogues covering Italian Renaissance and eighteenth-century painting.

Valagussa's most significant single publication is the Catalogo completo dei dipinti italiani del Trecento e Quattrocento dell'Accademia Carrara (Officina Libraria, 2018), the first volume of the museum's scientific catalogues. The work is notable for introducing a number of relevant new attributions and providing a complete bibliographic apparatus for the collection, making it an essential reference for anyone working on Italian medieval and early Renaissance painting. His attribution of a Virgin and Child (Forli, Pinacoteca Civica) to the Master of the Borgo Crucifix is among the scholarly contributions that have drawn international attention, cited even in the National Gallery's catalogues. Beyond the catalogue, he has published numerous essays and articles focusing primarily on medieval and Renaissance art.

Throughout his career, Valagussa has curated a wide range of major monographic exhibitions, including those dedicated to Fra Galgario, Giovanni Battista Moroni (Piccio), Carlo Ceresa, and Andrea Mantegna. In 2017, he co-curated the exhibition Serenissime Trame at the Ca' d'Oro in Venice. In 2021, he organized an exhibition of masterpieces from the Accademia Carrara in Shanghai, demonstrating the international reach of his curatorial work. He has also presented individual works for broader public audiences, including a presentation of Lorenzo Lotto's Madonna col Bambino e i santi Giovanni Battista e Caterina d'Alessandria for RAI Radio 3's Museo Nazionale.

Since January 2022, following the conclusion of his curatorial post, Valagussa has continued as a studioso associated with the Comune di Bergamo and remains a docente a contratto in the Department of History, Archaeology, and Art History at the Università Cattolica. He is described by those who know him as a reserved and modest person (una persona schiva, modesta), dedicated entirely to scholarship rather than public visibility. Married with two children, he has spent much of his professional life in the Bergamo area, contributing enormously to the visibility and scholarly reputation of one of northern Italy's most important picture galleries. His expertise in the attributional study of medieval Italian painting, shaped by the Boskovits and Longhi traditions, makes him a key reference figure for researchers working on Trecento and Quattrocento art today.

Vasari, Giorgio

Arezzo, 30 July 1511 – Florence, 27 June 1574

Giorgio Vasari was an Aretine painter, architect, courtier and writer whose Le vite de' più eccellenti pittori, scultori e architettori effectively founded early modern art history and shaped how later generations understood Italian medieval and Renaissance art.

Vasari was born in Arezzo on 30 July 1511 into an artisan family and showed precocious artistic talent. A cousin, Luca Signorelli, recommended the boy, and Vasari first trained locally with the French glass-painter Guglielmo da Marsiglia, absorbing a solid craft and drawing discipline. Around sixteen he was sent to Florence under the patronage of Cardinal Silvio Passerini, entering the circle of Andrea del Sarto and his pupils Rosso Fiorentino and Pontormo, and receiving a humanist education that included Latin authors and Tuscan literary culture. In the early 1530s he travelled to Rome, where he studied both ancient remains and the works of Raphael and Michelangelo; this encounter with the High Renaissance and antiquity decisively marked his taste and his theoretical conception of artistic progress.

These formative years were peripatetic: Vasari worked between Arezzo, Florence, Rome, Venice and other centres, executing altarpieces, devotional pictures and decorative cycles. His first securely dated work is the Burial of Christ (1532) now in Casa Vasari at Arezzo, already reflecting Medicean patronage and a Mannerist vocabulary indebted to Rosso and Salviati. Short stays in Venice (1541–42) brought him into contact with Venetian colour and with patrons such as the Corner-Spinelli, for whom he painted allegorical ceiling decorations and works like Patience, Justice and Hope. This early career built his reputation as a versatile decorator capable of handling large narrative and allegorical programs in a courtly idiom.

From the 1540s Vasari's career became increasingly tied to the Medici court. After various commissions in Rome and Naples, he returned definitively to Florence in the 1550s at the service of Duke Cosimo I, becoming the principal designer and organizer of large-scale decorative enterprises celebrating Medicean power. His painting is best represented by the vast cycles in the Palazzo Vecchio, where he and his workshop frescoed the Salone dei Cinquecento and other rooms with scenes exalting Florence and the Medici dynasty, as well as by the Cento giorni fresco in the Palazzo della Cancelleria in Rome, with episodes from the life of Paul III.

As an architect Vasari produced projects in several cities, but his most celebrated work is the Palazzo degli Uffizi in Florence, begun in 1560 as the administrative "offices" of Cosimo I. The long, narrow courtyard opening toward the Arno, with its regular rhythm of columns and loggias, is one of the iconic urban spaces of late Renaissance Florence. He also designed the Vasari Corridor (1565), the elevated passage linking Palazzo Vecchio to Palazzo Pitti via the Uffizi and Ponte Vecchio, a striking expression of courtly control over the city's space. Other architectural achievements include interventions in Santa Croce, the arrangement of artistic spaces in the Palazzo Vecchio, and work on fortifications and villas for Medicean patrons.

Vasari maintained a house in Florence and built a richly decorated residence in his native Arezzo (1547 onwards), where he frescoed walls and ceilings with allegories of the arts and scenes celebrating his own career; the Casa Vasari is now a museum and important document of his self-fashioning as artist and man of letters.

Despite his substantial artistic output, Vasari's enduring fame rests principally on his literary work, Le vite de' più eccellenti pittori, scultori e architettori. First published in Florence in 1550 and then in a greatly expanded, three-volume edition in 1568, the Lives offer a sequence of biographies from Cimabue and Giotto to Michelangelo, prefaced by theoretical introductions in which Vasari advances a teleological history of Italian art. He divides this history into three ages: an "infancy" with Cimabue and Giotto, a "youth" with masters such as Donatello, Brunelleschi, Ghiberti and Masaccio, and a "maturity" or "perfection" embodied by Leonardo, Raphael and above all Michelangelo.

The Lives are a hybrid work, combining anecdote, workshop gossip, documentary facts, stylistic judgments and theoretical reflections about design, imitation and artistic progress. Vasari consistently privileges drawing (disegno), primarily Florentine, as the root of artistic excellence and tends to view Venetian colour with some suspicion, although the second edition gives more space and praise to Venetian masters than the first. He frames the arts as moving from decline after antiquity, through a medieval "dark age", to "rebirth" in Tuscany — a period he literally calls rinascita, providing one of the fundamental early formulations of the concept of the Renaissance.

From a historiographical point of view, the Lives have long been considered "the first important book on art history" and "perhaps the most famous, and still the most read, work of early art literature". Modern scholarship has, of course, identified many factual errors, biases and hagiographic distortions, especially in biographies of artists before Vasari's own time or outside his Florentine milieu. Yet the work remains an indispensable primary source for fourteenth- to sixteenth-century Italian art, not only for its anecdotes and data but also as a key text in constructing the very narrative of artistic "progress" that dominated European art history for centuries. Vasari is cited throughout this database as an early written witness for works and attributions that must be weighed critically against documentary and material evidence.

Vasari's position at the Medici court made him a central cultural mediator between artists, patrons and institutions. In 1563, together with Cosimo I and with Michelangelo as symbolic head, he helped found the Florentine Accademia e Compagnia delle Arti del Disegno, one of the earliest artist academies in Europe, which codified the status of painting, sculpture and architecture as liberal arts based on disegno and theory rather than mere manual trades. This institutionalization of the profession is inseparable from the broader program of the Lives to present artists as heroic, intellectual figures comparable to poets and philosophers.

In his last decade Vasari continued to work for the Medici, supervising the decoration of spaces such as the interior of Brunelleschi's dome, where he designed the immense fresco cycle of the Last Judgment (begun 1572), completed after his death by Federico Zuccari. He also produced late paintings such as Perseus and Andromeda for Francesco I de' Medici, showing his continued engagement with mythological allegory and courtly themes. Giorgio Vasari died in Florence on 27 June 1574 and was buried in Santissima Annunziata; by then he was widely admired as a successful "Renaissance man" who united artistic practice, writing and architectural invention.

From the seventeenth century onward, scholars, collectors and artists repeatedly returned to the Lives for information and inspiration, and modern editions and translations have made it a constant presence in art-historical education. For this reason later historians have aptly called Vasari the "father of art history", not because he was neutral or "objective", but because he provided the first sustained, internally coherent narrative of the development of the visual arts, one that continues to structure debates about Giotto, Cimabue, Masaccio and their successors even when we argue against him.

Venturi, Adolfo

1856 – 1941

Adolfo Venturi was an Italian art historian and connoisseur who fundamentally shaped the modern study of medieval Italian painting. Through his monumental nine-volume Storia dell'arte italiana and meticulous connoisseurship, Venturi established the foundational protocols for attribution and chronological sequencing of Giunta Pisano's works. His detailed study of signed crucifix panels demonstrated that thirteenth-century panel paintings were products of extraordinary technical sophistication and profound theological intention rather than primitive precursors to the Renaissance.

Venturi's comparative analysis of surviving works established the chronological development of Giunta's stylistic evolution and positioned him not as a marginal medieval craftsman but as a revolutionary figure whose innovations fundamentally reoriented medieval art scholarship and inaugurated the artistic transformations culminating in the Renaissance.

Venturi, Lionello

Modena, 25 April 1885 – Rome, 14 August 1961

Lionello Venturi was one of the greatest Italian art historians and critics of the twentieth century, distinguished both by the breadth and originality of his scholarship and by the civic courage with which he opposed fascism, refusing allegiance to the regime even at the cost of exile. His work spans medieval, Renaissance, and modern art, and remains foundational for the international historiography of art criticism.

Born in Modena to Adolfo Venturi and Giovanna Zanni, he grew up in an intellectually formative environment and moved early to Rome with his family. He graduated in Literature and Philosophy at La Sapienza in 1907 and in 1911 obtained his qualification to teach medieval and modern art history at the University of Padua. After graduation, he held a rapid sequence of museum and administrative posts: inspector of the Venice Galleries (1909-1910), inspector of the Galleria Borghese in Rome (1911-1912), and, at only twenty-eight, Director and Superintendent of the National Gallery of Urbino (1913-1914).

Between 1914 and 1915 he entered university teaching at Turin, where he was soon appointed associate professor. With Italy's entry into World War I he volunteered for military service; wounded in combat in 1917, he was discharged with a decoration for military valor. From 1915 to 1931 he held the chair of Art History at the University of Turin, where he trained a major generation of students including Giulio Carlo Argan and Mario Soldati. His teaching combined methodological rigor with sustained attention to both the aesthetic quality of the work and its historical context.

In October 1931, Venturi was among the very small group of Italian university professors who refused the oath of allegiance imposed by the fascist regime. The decision led to his immediate exclusion from university teaching in Italy. In 1932 he left for Paris, where he remained until 1939, developing contacts with the anti-fascist movement Giustizia e Libertà and teaching and lecturing in Paris, Lyon, London, and Cambridge. After the introduction of racial laws, he promoted the Italian section of the International League Against Racism and Anti-Semitism. With the outbreak of World War II he moved to New York (1939), taught at American universities, took leadership roles in the Mazzini Society, and chaired the Italian Emergency Rescue Committee.

His years in exile were also among his most productive intellectually. Major works include Histoire de la critique d'art (1936; expanded Italian edition, 1948), still a central text in the history of art criticism; the two-volume Cezanne (1936), which established him as a leading interpreter of modern painting; Archives de l'Impressionisme (2 vols., 1939), a key documentary corpus for Impressionist studies; and Come si guarda un quadro, one of the most widely read twentieth-century Italian books on aesthetic education. For medieval and Renaissance studies, Il gusto dei primitivi (1926) remains especially significant for the reassessment of early Italian painting.

After the Liberation, Venturi returned to Italy in 1945 and resumed teaching at La Sapienza, remaining there until 1955 and contributing decisively to the reconstruction of postwar Italian art-historical culture. He was elected to the Accademia dei Lincei, the Accademia di San Luca, and the Accademia delle Scienze di Torino, and became a corresponding member of the Institut de France. He died in Rome on 14 August 1961.

Vittorini, Marta

L'Aquila, c. 1975-1976; active 21st century

Marta Vittorini is an Italian scholar, archivist, and medievalist from L'Aquila, currently serving as Director of the Archivio di Stato dell'Aquila. Her profile bridges academic research in medieval philosophy and manuscript studies with institutional leadership in the preservation and enhancement of Italy's archival and bibliographic heritage.

Vittorini studied Philosophy at the Università degli Studi dell'Aquila (Univaq), graduating with 110/110 con lode. Her thesis, Il problema dell'onnipotenza divina tra il XIII e il XIV secolo: Tommaso, Scoto e Ockham in dibattito, addressed a core issue of late medieval theology and anticipated her sustained engagement with scholastic thought.

She later completed a doctorate in Medieval Philosophy with the dissertation Predicabili e categorie nell'ultimo commento di Walter Burley all'Isagoge di Porfirio, including a critical edition based on three principal manuscripts. This work placed her within high-level textual scholarship on late medieval Aristotelianism, with research interests centered on theology, metaphysics, and philosophy of nature in the late Middle Ages.

Alongside philosophical training, Vittorini pursued advanced specialization in the material study of manuscripts. She obtained the Diploma di Archivista Paleografo at the Scuola Vaticana di Paleografia, Diplomatica e Archivistica, where her thesis examined the manuscript tradition of Walter Burley in codices of the Biblioteca Apostolica Vaticana. She also earned a Diploma di Conservatore di Manoscritti at Università La Sapienza di Roma, strengthening her expertise in manuscript conservation.

Her disciplinary range includes Latin and Greek paleography, codicology, history of manuscript illumination (storia della miniatura), history of manuscript transmission, library science, and cultural heritage legislation. She additionally attended Arabic language training and a seminar on Arabic paleography and codicology taught by Adam Gacek (McGill University), extending her methodological competence toward Arabic manuscript traditions.

The central focus of Vittorini's scholarly research is Walter Burley (c. 1274-c. 1344), the English scholastic philosopher and commentator on Aristotle. Her work analyzes Burley's logic and metaphysics through both philosophical argument and manuscript evidence, integrating history of ideas with codicological investigation.

She presented this research in international venues, including the XII International Congress of Medieval Philosophy (SIEPM, Palermo, 2007) with the paper Some Notes about the Metaphysical Composition of Individuals in Walter Burley. She also delivered Walter Burley on the Role of Difference at the Scuola Normale Superiore and presented at the Pisa workshop Conoscenza e contingenza nella tradizione aristotelica medievale (Convento di Santa Croce in Fossabanda, 2006).

In July 2007 she took part in the Workshop in Editing Medieval Texts at Georgetown University (Washington, D.C.) and later completed a two-month research stage at the Biblioteca dei Gesuiti in Salamanca, further consolidating her editorial and manuscript-based methods.

From February 2018 to December 2021, Vittorini served as Funzionario Archivista di Stato (Area III, F1) at the Soprintendenza in Rome, where she handled declarations of historical interest, authorizations for restoration and archival arrangement, and supervision of archives belonging to major institutions, including Confindustria, Confartigianato, INAIL, and sectors of the antiquarian market.

On 1 January 2022, by decree of the Director General of Archives (rep. 898/2021), she was appointed Director of the Archivio di Stato dell'Aquila, succeeding Sebastiana Ferrari. In this role she oversees the three archive sites in L'Aquila, Avezzano, and Sulmona, with responsibility for staff, conservation of archival and bibliographic patrimony, maintenance, and public services. She has also promoted the institute's collections through exhibitions, web communication, and social media, and collaborates with the Carabinieri TPC unit on expert evaluations of archival and bibliographic materials under investigation.

Vittorini has maintained an active publication record in medieval philosophy, manuscript studies, archival law, and the protection of non-state archives, including a 2023 contribution on the duties of Soprintendenze archivistiche e bibliografiche. She has also authored a book-interview with Professor Colapietra, reflecting her engagement with the wider cultural life of Abruzzo. Her profile exemplifies the intersection of medieval scholarship, technical manuscript expertise, and institutional leadership in the safeguarding of documentary heritage.

Volpe, Carlo

Bologna, 11 giugno 1926 - Bologna, 2 febbraio 1984

Carlo Volpe è stato uno dei più influenti storici dell'arte italiani del secondo Novecento, formatosi alla scuola di Roberto Longhi e protagonista degli studi sul Duecento e Trecento, in particolare sul Trecento riminese e bolognese.

Volpe nasce a Bologna nel 1926 e cresce in un ambiente che lo porta presto a confrontarsi con la grande tradizione degli studi storico-artistici italiani. La sua formazione decisiva avviene all'Università di Bologna, dove è allievo di Roberto Longhi, del quale assimila il metodo filologico basato sul confronto diretto con le opere, l'attenzione alla qualità visiva e l'uso serrato della fotografia come strumento di indagine. Nel novembre del 1949 discute con Longhi una tesi dedicata a Pietro Lorenzetti ad Assisi, che già rivela il suo interesse per la pittura trecentesca e per i rapporti fra scuole regionali. In questi anni si forma anche nel clima longhiano delle riviste e della critica militante, che mette in dialogo antico e contemporaneo.

Poco dopo la laurea, Volpe comincia a collaborare al Nuovo Corriere con interventi e recensioni che spaziano dall'arte antica alle ricerche contemporanee, mostrando una curiosità ampia e non settoriale. Parallelamente, entra nel gruppo di giovani redattori legati all'esperienza di Paragone, la rivista fondata da Longhi, dove debutta nel 1951 con un saggio su Ambrogio Lorenzetti e i rapporti fra Firenze e Siena nel quarto decennio del Trecento. Questa precoce attività critica lo pone subito in dialogo con figure come Francesco Arcangeli, Giuliano Briganti e Mina Gregori, con cui condivide una riflessione comune sul metodo e sui confini della storia dell'arte.

Dopo un lungo periodo di insegnamenti "secondari" presso l'Istituto di Storia dell'arte dell'Università di Bologna, Volpe ottiene nel 1974 la cattedra di Storia dell'arte medievale e moderna. Il suo insegnamento si colloca in continuità con la lezione longhiana ma introduce una particolare attenzione alla sistematicità degli studi sul Trecento e sulla tradizione emiliana, contribuendo a formare una generazione di storici dell'arte sensibili al dato visivo e, insieme, alle strutture critiche di lungo periodo. Nel 1982 diventa il primo direttore del neonato Dipartimento delle Arti Visive dell'Ateneo bolognese, trasformando l'eredità dell'Istituto di Storia dell'arte in una struttura dipartimentale più ampia, che integra storia dell'arte, studi visivi e nuove metodologie.
